📡 Part 1: Foundation — Physical & Data Link LayersCh 1-4

Chapter 1: What Is Networking? (The Big Picture)

Before diving into cables, packets, and protocols, let's understand why networking matters for DevOps, Platform Engineering, and daily operations. We'll use our anihpj/jobpost Django app — deployed with Docker and orchestrated on Kubernetes — to make every concept concrete and relevant to your day-to-day work.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 📖 Concepts 🎯 Foundation

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Why networking is the #1 skill for troubleshooting production incidents — "It's always DNS... or the network"
  • ✅ The layered observability mindset: start at the bottom and work up
  • ✅ The OSI Model — all 7 layers, what each does, and which tools inspect each layer
  • ✅ Encapsulation — how data flows through the network stack like a Russian nesting doll
  • ✅ The TCP/IP Model — the 4-layer model the Internet actually runs on
  • ✅ Essential networking tools every DevOps/Platform Engineer uses daily

1.1 Why Networking Matters for DevOps & Platform Engineers

Every production outage has a network dimension. When a user reports "the site is down" or "jobpost.anihpj.com is timing out" — where do you start? Is it the application code? The database? DNS? A firewall rule? A misconfigured Kubernetes service?

⚠️ GOTCHA: "Ping Works but Curl Doesn't"

A common trap: You ping the server and it responds, so you think "the network is fine." This is wrong. ICMP (ping) is often allowed through firewalls while TCP ports (like 8000 for your Django app) are blocked. Always test the actual protocol and port your application uses.

The Layered Observability Mindset — Start at the Bottom, Work Up
  🔍 NETWORKING = LAYERED OBSERVABILITY

  Layer 7 (Application):  "Is the app responding?"  → curl, HTTP status codes
  Layer 4 (Transport):    "Is the port open?"        → nc -zv, ss -tlnp, telnet
  Layer 3 (Network):      "Can I reach it?"           → ping, traceroute, ip route
  Layer 2 (Data Link):    "Is ARP resolving?"         → ip neigh show, arping
  Layer 1 (Physical):     "Is the link up?"           → ip link show, ethtool

  🥇 Golden Rule:  START AT THE BOTTOM AND WORK UP
  🥈 Second Rule:  THE PACKETS DON'T LIE. Trust tcpdump.

Real Incident: "Users Can't Reach jobpost.anihpj.com"

Here's a real scenario you'll face. A user reports the anihpj job board is inaccessible. Your debugging workflow:

🔴 User Reports

"Site is down!"

→
🧠 Isolate

Network? App? DNS? DB?

→
🔍 L1 Check

Is interface up?

→
🔍 L2 Check

ARP resolving?

→
🔍 L3 Check

ping works?

→
🔍 L4 Check

Port 8000 open?

→
🔍 L7 Check

HTTP 200?

→
✅ Fixed!

Root cause found

😰 Managing anihpj WITHOUT Network Understanding
  • 🔴 User says "site is down" → you restart the Django pod → still broken → restart the database → still broken → panic
  • 🔴 You can't tell if it's a DNS issue, a firewall rule, or a crashed container
  • 🔴 Every incident takes hours because you're guessing, not systematically debugging
🎉 Managing anihpj WITH Network Understanding
  • ✅ User says "site is down" → you check L1→L7 systematically → find the DNS misconfiguration in 5 minutes
  • ✅ You know exactly which tool to use at each layer: ip link, ping, nc -zv, curl -v, dig
  • ✅ You confidently tell your team: "It's a CoreDNS issue, not the application."

Real Incident Walk-Through: "The Site Is Down"

Let's trace through a real production incident with the anihpj job board. This is exactly how an experienced engineer approaches a network issue — methodically, layer by layer, letting the tools guide the investigation.

Incident: Users Report jobpost.anihpj.com Is Unreachable
  🚨 9:15 AM — PagerDuty Alert: "HTTP 500 errors from anihpj-prod"

  STEP 1 — L1 (Physical):     ssh node-1 → ip link show eth0
      RESULT: state UP, 1000 Mbps full duplex ✅ — Physical layer OK

  STEP 2 — L2 (Data Link):    ip neigh show | grep 10.0.1.1 (gateway)
      RESULT: REACHABLE ✅ — ARP resolving, L2 OK

  STEP 3 — L3 (Network):      ping -c 3 10.244.2.10 (pod IP)
      RESULT: 3/3 received, 1ms avg ✅ — IP routing OK

  STEP 4 — L4 (Transport):    nc -zv 10.244.2.10 8000
      RESULT: Connection refused ❌ — PORT ISSUE!

  🔍 INVESTIGATION:
      kubectl get pods -n anihpj → pod STATUS: CrashLoopBackOff
      kubectl logs anihpj-pod → "django.db.utils.OperationalError:
      could not connect to server: Connection refused"
      → Database pod had restarted with a new IP, but Django
        was still using the OLD IP from its connection pool!

  🔧 FIX:
      kubectl rollout restart deployment/anihpj
      → Django restarts, re-resolves DB hostname → site back up!

  ⏱️ TOTAL TIME: 7 minutes (vs 45+ minutes of random guessing)
💡 Key Takeaway: Systematic vs Random Debugging

The difference between a junior engineer and a senior engineer isn't knowledge — it's methodology. A junior restarts random pods and hopes. A senior starts at Layer 1 and works up, checking each layer once. The systematic approach is faster, more reliable, and doesn't make the problem worse by restarting things unnecessarily.

1.2 The OSI Model — Networking's Periodic Table

The Open Systems Interconnection (OSI) model is the universal framework for understanding how data moves across networks. It breaks networking into 7 distinct layers — each with a specific job. Think of it as networking's periodic table: once you understand the layers, everything else falls into place.

📖 Historical Context

The OSI model was developed in the 1970s by the International Organization for Standardization (ISO). While the OSI protocols never gained wide adoption, the OSI model became the standard teaching framework. The Internet actually runs on the simpler TCP/IP model (4 layers), which we'll cover in Section 1.4.

⚠️ GOTCHA: OSI is a Teaching Tool

The OSI model is a conceptual framework, not a blueprint. The Internet runs on the TCP/IP model (4 layers). Don't obsess over which exact OSI layer a protocol belongs to — focus on understanding which layer your problem is at so you know which tool to reach for.

The 7 Layers — Complete Reference Table

OSI 7-Layer Model — Full Reference with anihpj Examples
  ┌─────┬────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ LYR │      NAME      │       FUNCTION           │   PROTOCOLS    │    ANIHPJ EXAMPLE         │
  ├─────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │  7  │  Application   │ User-facing services     │ HTTP, DNS, SSH │ jobpost API returns JSON  │
  │     │                │                          │                │ at /api/jobs/             │
  ├─────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │  6  │  Presentation  │ Data format & encryption │ TLS, SSL, JPEG │ HTTPS cert on K8s ingress │
  ├─────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │  5  │  Session       │ Connection management    │ NetBIOS, RPC   │ Django session cookies    │
  ├─────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │  4  │  Transport     │ Reliable delivery        │ TCP, UDP       │ Port 8000 (gunicorn)      │
  ├─────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │  3  │  Network       │ Routing & addressing     │ IP, ICMP       │ 10.244.1.5 → pod IP      │
  ├─────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │  2  │  Data Link     │ Hop-to-hop delivery      │ Ethernet, ARP  │ MAC addr of K8s worker    │
  ├─────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │  1  │  Physical      │ Bits on the wire         │ 1000BASE-T     │ Cat6 cable to switch      │
  └─────┴────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘
💡 Mnemonic: "Please Do Not Throw Sausage Pizza Away"

Physical → Data Link → Network → Transport → Session → Presentation → Application. Reading from bottom (Layer 1) to top (Layer 7).

Each Layer's Job in ONE Sentence + The Tool That Inspects It

⚡
Layer 1: Physical

Job: Move raw bits across a physical medium (copper, fiber, radio).
Tool: ethtool, ip link show
Question: "Is the link up?"

🔗
Layer 2: Data Link

Job: Deliver frames between devices on the SAME network.
Tool: ip neigh show, arping
Question: "Is ARP resolving?"

🌍
Layer 3: Network

Job: Route packets across DIFFERENT networks using IP.
Tool: ping, traceroute, ip route
Question: "Can I reach the destination?"

🚚
Layer 4: Transport

Job: Deliver data reliably (TCP) or quickly (UDP) to the right application.
Tool: ss -tlnp, nc -zv
Question: "Is the port open?"

🤝
Layer 5: Session

Job: Manage connections between applications.
Tool: ss -tn state established
Question: "Is the session active?"

🔐
Layer 6: Presentation

Job: Format, encrypt, and compress data.
Tool: openssl s_client
Question: "Is TLS working?"

📱
Layer 7: Application

Job: Provide services to end users.
Tool: curl -v, dig
Question: "Is the app responding?"

1.3 Encapsulation — The Russian Doll of Networking

Encapsulation is the process of wrapping data with protocol headers at each layer. Data flows DOWN the stack when sending (headers get ADDED) and UP the stack when receiving (headers get STRIPPED). Each layer only looks at its own header.

Encapsulation Walk-Through — anihpj GET /api/jobs/ Request
  🖥️ SENDING (anihpj Pod)                          📡 RECEIVING (User's Browser)
  ═══════════════════                            ═══════════════════════

  Layer 7: HTTP GET /api/jobs/                   Layer 7: HTTP GET /api/jobs/
      │         ▲                                      ▲         │
      ▼         │                                      │         ▼
  Layer 4: +TCP Header (port 8000)               Layer 4: -TCP Header
      │         ▲                                      ▲         │
      ▼         │                                      │         ▼
  Layer 3: +IP Header (src: 10.244.1.5)          Layer 3: -IP Header
      │         ▲                                      ▲         │
      ▼         │                                      │         ▼
  Layer 2: +Ethernet Frame (MAC addr)            Layer 2: -Ethernet Frame
      │         ▲                                      ▲         │
      ▼         │                                      │         ▼
  Layer 1: 10110010... (bits on wire)  ═══════▶  Layer 1: 10110010...

  📦 Each layer WRAPS the one above it — like Russian nesting dolls 🪆
⚠️ GOTCHA: Same Bytes, Different Perspective

When you see "TCP payload" in a packet capture, it's the HTTP request. When you see "IP payload," it's the TCP segment. They're the exact same bytes — just viewed from different layers. This is why tcpdump output at different layers looks different but represents the same data.

Simplified: HTTP Inside TCP Inside IP Inside Ethernet
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🟦 ETHERNET FRAME                                   │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  🟩 IP PACKET                                  │  │
  │  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐  │  │
  │  │  │  🟨 TCP SEGMENT                          │  │  │
  │  │  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────┐  │  │  │
  │  │  │  │  🟥 HTTP REQUEST                   │  │  │  │
  │  │  │  │  GET /api/jobs/ HTTP/1.1           │  │  │  │
  │  │  │  │  Host: jobpost.anihpj.com          │  │  │  │
  │  │  │  └───────────────────────────────────┘  │  │  │
  │  │  │  TCP Header: src_port=8000, dst=443     │  │  │
  │  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────┘  │  │
  │  │  IP Header: src=10.244.1.5, dst=203.0.113.1   │  │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │  Ethernet: src_mac=aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff, dst=gateway  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

1.4 The TCP/IP Model — What We Actually Use

While the OSI model has 7 layers, the Internet runs on the TCP/IP model with just 4 layers. TCP/IP was developed alongside the Internet itself — it's simpler, practical, and actually deployed everywhere. OSI layers 5-7 all collapse into TCP/IP's single "Application" layer.

OSI (7 Layers) vs TCP/IP (4 Layers) — Side-by-Side Comparison
  ┌─────────────────────────┐        ┌─────────────────────────────┐
  │     OSI MODEL (7)       │        │     TCP/IP MODEL (4)        │
  ├─────────────────────────┤        ├─────────────────────────────┤
  │                         │        │                             │
  │  7. Application  ───────┼──┐     │                             │
  │  6. Presentation ───────┼──┤     │  APPLICATION               │
  │  5. Session      ───────┼──┘     │  (HTTP, DNS, TLS, SSH,     │
  │                         │        │   SMTP, FTP, gRPC)          │
  ├─────────────────────────┤        ├─────────────────────────────┤
  │  4. Transport    ───────┼───────▶│  TRANSPORT                  │
  │                         │        │  (TCP, UDP, SCTP)           │
  ├─────────────────────────┤        ├─────────────────────────────┤
  │  3. Network      ───────┼───────▶│  INTERNET                   │
  │                         │        │  (IP, ICMP, IPSec)          │
  ├─────────────────────────┤        ├─────────────────────────────┤
  │  2. Data Link    ───────┼──┐     │  LINK / NETWORK ACCESS      │
  │  1. Physical     ───────┼──┘     │  (Ethernet, ARP, Wi-Fi,     │
  │                         │        │   PPP, VLAN)                │
  └─────────────────────────┘        └─────────────────────────────┘

Why TCP/IP Won and OSI Stayed Theoretical

💰
TCP/IP Was Free

Developed with U.S. government funding (DARPA), TCP/IP was freely available. OSI standards were expensive to purchase and implement.

🚀
TCP/IP Was Working

By the time OSI standards were finalized (1984), TCP/IP was already running the ARPANET (predecessor to the Internet).

📦
TCP/IP Was Simpler

4 layers vs 7. The simpler model was easier to implement and deploy. OSI over-engineered layers 5-6.

🏆
Network Effect

More people used TCP/IP → more tools built for it → even more people used it. OSI couldn't catch up.

1.5 Networking in the DevOps Toolchain

These are the tools you'll use every single day as a DevOps/Platform Engineer. Each one maps to a specific OSI layer — knowing which tool to reach for is half the battle in troubleshooting.

Essential Networking Tools — Your Daily Arsenal
ToolLayerWhat It DoesExample (anihpj)
curlL7HTTP client — test APIs and web endpointscurl -v https://jobpost.anihpj.com/api/jobs/
digL7DNS lookup — resolve domain names to IPsdig jobpost.anihpj.com
openssl s_clientL6TLS/SSL inspection — check certificatesopenssl s_client -connect anihpj.com:443
ssL4Socket statistics — see listening portsss -tlnp | grep 8000
nc (netcat)L4TCP/UDP connection testernc -zv 10.244.1.5 8000
pingL3ICMP echo — test basic reachabilityping -c 4 10.244.1.5
tracerouteL3Map the path packets taketraceroute jobpost.anihpj.com
ip routeL3View/manage routing tableip route get 10.244.1.5
tcpdumpL2-L7Packet capture — THE truth serumtcpdump -i eth0 port 8000
arpingL2ARP resolution testarping -I eth0 10.0.1.1
ip neighL2View ARP cacheip neigh show
ethtoolL1NIC diagnosticsethtool eth0
ip linkL1Interface state (up/down)ip link show eth0
💡 Pro Tip: Build Your "First 5 Minutes" Checklist

For ANY network incident, run these commands first (in this order):

  • ip link show — Is the interface UP?
  • ip neigh show — Can we resolve MAC addresses?
  • ping -c 3 <target> — Is IP reachable?
  • ss -tlnp | grep <port> — Is the application listening?
  • curl -v <url> — Is the app responding correctly?

This 5-step checklist will solve 80% of your daily network issues before you even open a browser.

📝 Chapter 1 — Networking Practice Questions

Q1
A user reports that jobpost.anihpj.com is "down." You can ping the server successfully but curl https://jobpost.anihpj.com returns Connection refused. At which OSI layer is the problem MOST likely occurring?
Show Answer
Answer

Layer 4 (Transport) — or Layer 7 (Application). A successful ping proves Layers 1-3 are working. Connection refused means the TCP three-way handshake reached the server but no application is listening on that port. Check: ss -tlnp | grep 443 to see if the web server is actually running.

Explanation

This is the classic "ping works but the app doesn't" scenario. ICMP (ping) at Layer 3 bypasses TCP entirely. Always test the specific protocol and port your application uses — never trust ping alone as proof the "network is fine."

Q2
List the 7 layers of the OSI model from bottom (Layer 1) to top (Layer 7) and name one protocol that operates at each layer.
Show Answer
Answer
L1 PhysicalEthernet (1000BASE-T), Wi-Fi (802.11)
L2 Data LinkARP, Ethernet frames, VLAN (802.1Q)
L3 NetworkIP (IPv4/IPv6), ICMP
L4 TransportTCP, UDP
L5 SessionNetBIOS, RPC, SOCKS
L6 PresentationTLS/SSL, JPEG, ASCII
L7 ApplicationHTTP, DNS, SSH, SMTP
Explanation

Remember the mnemonic: Please Do Not Throw Sausage Pizza Away. Knowing which protocol runs at which layer helps you immediately identify which tools to use when troubleshooting.

Q3
Explain encapsulation. When you run curl https://anihpj.com/api/jobs/, describe how the data flows through the OSI layers on the sending side.
Show Answer
Answer

Data flows DOWN the stack, with each layer adding its own header:

L7: HTTP GET /api/jobs/ Host: anihpj.com
L6: TLS encrypts the HTTP data
L4: TCP adds src_port, dst_port=443, seq numbers
L3: IP adds src_ip, dst_ip
L2: Ethernet adds src_mac, dst_mac (default gateway)
L1: Bits transmitted on the wire
Explanation

Think of it as Russian nesting dolls 🪆. Each layer wraps the data from the layer above. On the receiving side, the process reverses — each layer strips its header and passes the payload up.

Q4
What are the 4 layers of the TCP/IP model, and how do they map to the 7-layer OSI model?
Show Answer
Answer
TCP/IP LayerOSI LayersKey Protocols
Application5+6+7HTTP, DNS, TLS, SSH
Transport4TCP, UDP
Internet3IP, ICMP
Link/Network Access1+2Ethernet, ARP, Wi-Fi
Explanation

The TCP/IP model collapses OSI's Session (5), Presentation (6), and Application (7) into a single "Application" layer. It also combines Physical (1) and Data Link (2) into "Link." This is the model the Internet actually uses — OSI is primarily a teaching framework.

Q5
You're debugging why your Django app (anihpj) running in a Kubernetes pod can't reach the PostgreSQL database. List the tools you would use at each layer to diagnose the issue, bottom-up.
Show Answer
Answer
# L1: Is the pod's network interface up?
kubectl exec anihpj-pod -- ip link show eth0

# L2: Can we resolve the DB service MAC?
kubectl exec anihpj-pod -- ip neigh show

# L3: Can we reach the DB pod IP?
kubectl exec anihpj-pod -- ping -c 3 10.244.2.10

# L4: Is PostgreSQL listening on port 5432?
kubectl exec anihpj-pod -- nc -zv postgres-svc 5432

# L7: Can we query the database?
kubectl exec anihpj-pod -- python manage.py dbshell
Explanation

Always start at the bottom (L1) and work up. This systematic approach prevents you from wasting time restarting the application when the problem is actually a network policy blocking traffic at L3/L4. In Kubernetes, kubectl exec is your gateway into the pod's network namespace.

Q6
What is the key difference between the OSI model and the TCP/IP model in terms of layers? Why did TCP/IP become the standard?
Show Answer
Answer

OSI has 7 layers; TCP/IP has 4. TCP/IP won because: (1) it was free (DARPA-funded), (2) it was already running the ARPANET when OSI standards were finalized, (3) it's simpler to implement (4 layers vs 7), and (4) network effects — more users meant more tools and adoption.

Explanation

This is a common interview question. The key insight: OSI was designed by committee before real-world deployment; TCP/IP evolved alongside the Internet. In practice, the OSI model is still invaluable for teaching and troubleshooting — but the TCP/IP protocols run the world.

Q7
A colleague says "I can ping the server, so the network is fine." Explain why this statement can be dangerously misleading, using the OSI model.
Show Answer
Answer

ping (ICMP) only tests Layers 1-3. A successful ping proves the physical link (L1), Ethernet/ARP (L2), and IP routing (L3) work. It tells you NOTHING about: TCP ports being open (L4), TLS certificates being valid (L6), or the application responding correctly (L7). Firewalls often allow ICMP while blocking TCP ports — so the app can be completely unreachable even though ping succeeds.

Explanation

This is the single most common networking misconception. Always test at the layer your application operates at. For web apps: use curl -v. For databases: use nc -zv host port. Never trust ping alone.

Q8
Match each tool to its primary OSI layer: tcpdump, curl, ping, ss, dig, ethtool, arping
Show Answer
Answer
ethtoolL1 (Physical)NIC diagnostics, link state
arpingL2 (Data Link)ARP resolution test
pingL3 (Network)IP reachability via ICMP
ssL4 (Transport)Socket statistics, ports
curlL7 (Application)HTTP client, API testing
digL7 (Application)DNS queries
tcpdumpL2-L7 (ALL)Packet capture at any layer
Explanation

Knowing which tool maps to which layer means you can immediately reach for the right tool during an incident. tcpdump is unique — it captures at the data link layer (L2) but can inspect all layers above.

Q9
In the TCP/IP model, which layers are combined compared to the OSI model, and why does this combination make practical sense?
Show Answer
Answer

TCP/IP combines OSI L5+L6+L7 → "Application" and L1+L2 → "Link." The Application combination makes sense because in real implementations, session management, encryption, and application logic are often handled by the same software (e.g., a web browser handles HTTP, TLS, and session cookies together). L1+L2 combination reflects that Ethernet handles both physical signaling and framing together in practice.

Explanation

OSI's L5 (Session) and L6 (Presentation) were theoretical constructs that never materialized as separate protocols. In reality, TLS doesn't exist independently — it's always paired with an application protocol (HTTPS = HTTP + TLS).

Q10
You deployed anihpj on Kubernetes with a ClusterIP Service. From your laptop, you can't reach the app. From within the cluster, curl anihpj-svc:8000 works. Walk through your bottom-up debugging process and identify which layer the issue is at.
Show Answer
Answer
# L1-L3: Can I reach the K8s node? (from laptop)
ping <k8s-node-ip>  # ✅ Works → L1-L3 OK

# L4: Is the NodePort/Ingress port open?
nc -zv <k8s-node-ip> 30080  # ❌ Connection refused
# → L4 issue: No NodePort or Ingress exposing the service externally

# Solution: ClusterIP is internal-only!
# Create a NodePort Service or Ingress to expose externally:
kubectl expose deployment anihpj --type=NodePort --port=8000
Explanation

ClusterIP Services are only reachable from within the cluster. This is a Layer 4 (Transport) issue — the port isn't open from outside because no external-facing Service type (NodePort/LoadBalancer) or Ingress has been configured. This is one of the most common Kubernetes networking mistakes.

Chapter 2: Layer 1 — The Physical Layer (Bits on the Wire)

Layer 1 is the foundation of everything. If the physical connection isn't working, nothing else matters. We'll explore Ethernet — the protocol that has dominated LAN networking for 40+ years — along with MAC addresses, switches, VLANs, and ARP. Every concept is anchored to real commands you'll run on your Linux servers and Kubernetes nodes.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 📖 Hardware + L2 🔧 Hands-On

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ What actually travels through a cable — voltage, light, and radio waves
  • ✅ Ethernet frame anatomy — every field, byte by byte
  • ✅ MAC addresses — unicast, multicast, broadcast, and OUI lookup
  • ✅ How switches learn, forward, and flood — the 3 F's of L2
  • ✅ VLANs — splitting physical switches into logical networks with 802.1Q
  • ✅ ARP — how IP addresses resolve to MAC addresses, and common ARP pitfalls

2.1 What Actually Travels Through a Cable?

At the physical layer, data is just signaling — voltage changes on copper, light pulses on fiber, or radio waves through the air. The NIC converts 1s and 0s into these physical signals using an encoding scheme (Manchester, NRZ, PAM4).

From Bits to Signals — How Data Leaves Your NIC
  💻 APPLICATION:  "GET /api/jobs/ HTTP/1.1"
        ↓
  🧠 KERNEL:       TCP → IP → Ethernet Frame
        ↓
  🔌 NIC:          Encodes bits as voltage transitions
        ↓
  🔌 CABLE:        1011001011100101... (Manchester encoding)
        ↓
  🔌 SWITCH:       Reads voltage → decodes back to bits

  🔧 CHECK: ip link show eth0 → state UP/DOWN
           ethtool eth0 → Speed: 1000Mb/s, Duplex: Full, Link detected: yes
⚠️ GOTCHA: UP ≠ Healthy

A link can be "UP" at L1 but have 90% packet loss from a bad cable, duplex mismatch, or EMI interference. Always check ip -s link show eth0 for error counters — RX errors, dropped packets, and overruns tell the real story.

2.2 Ethernet — The Universal LAN Protocol

Ethernet has dominated LAN networking for over 40 years. Its secret? Simplicity, low cost, and backward compatibility — a 1990 10 Mbps NIC can still communicate with a 2025 400 Gbps switch.

🐌
10 Mbps

1980s — Coaxial cable, bus topology

🐢
100 Mbps

1995 — Fast Ethernet, Cat5, star topology

🐇
1 Gbps

1999 — Gigabit Ethernet, Cat5e/Cat6

🚀
10 Gbps

2006 — Data center standard, SFP+ optics

⚡
25-100 Gbps

2016+ — Cloud DC, 25G per lane, QSFP28

💫
400 Gbps

2022+ — Hyperscale DC, QSFP-DD, PAM4

Ethernet Frame Anatomy — Every Field Explained
  ┌────────┬──────────┬──────────┬────────────┬──────────────────┬──────┐
  │Preamble│ Dest MAC │ Src MAC  │ EtherType  │     PAYLOAD      │ FCS  │
  │ 8 bytes│  6 bytes │  6 bytes │  2 bytes   │   46-1500 bytes  │4 B   │
  ├────────┼──────────┼──────────┼────────────┼──────────────────┼──────┤
  │Sync +  │"Who gets│"Who sent │0x0800=IPv4 │ DATA (IP packet) │CRC32 │
  │frame   │ this?"  │ this?"   │0x0806=ARP  │ Min 46, Max 1500 │check │
  │delim   │         │         │0x86DD=IPv6│ (without VLAN tag)│      │
  └────────┴──────────┴──────────┴────────────┴──────────────────┴──────┘
⚠️ GOTCHA: VXLAN Overhead & MTU

Docker overlay networks add VXLAN overhead (50 bytes). If your physical NIC MTU is 1500, container MTU must be 1450 or less — or large packets get silently dropped. This is called a PMTU black hole. In Kubernetes with CNI plugins like Flannel/Calico using VXLAN, always set node MTU ≥ 1550 or configure the CNI to use a smaller pod MTU.

How Ethernet Handles Collisions: CSMA/CD (Historical Context)

In the original Ethernet design (shared coaxial cable), multiple devices shared the same physical wire. When two devices transmitted simultaneously, a collision occurred — both signals became garbled. Ethernet solved this with CSMA/CD (Carrier Sense Multiple Access with Collision Detection):

CSMA/CD — How Ethernet Avoided Chaos on Shared Media
  1. CARRIER SENSE:  Listen first — is anyone else transmitting?
                     If the wire is quiet, start sending.

  2. MULTIPLE ACCESS: Multiple stations share the same medium.
                     Anyone can transmit when the wire is free.

  3. COLLISION DETECT: While transmitting, also LISTEN.
                     If what you hear ≠ what you sent → COLLISION!

  4. JAM SIGNAL:     Send a 32-bit jam signal to ensure everyone
                     detects the collision.

  5. BACKOFF:        Wait a RANDOM time (binary exponential backoff),
                     then retry. Randomness prevents repeat collisions.

  ⚡ MODERN ETHERNET: Full-duplex + switches eliminated collisions
     entirely. CSMA/CD is historical but explains why Ethernet has
     minimum frame sizes (64 bytes) and why collision domains matter.

Half-Duplex vs Full-Duplex — Why It Matters

AspectHalf-DuplexFull-Duplex
TransmissionOne direction at a timeBoth directions simultaneously
CollisionsPossible (CSMA/CD needed)Impossible (separate TX/RX pairs)
Throughput~50-60% of rated speed~95-100% of rated speed
Typical UseWi-Fi (shared medium)Wired Ethernet (switched)
Duplex Mismatch⚠️ One side half, other full = LATE COLLISIONS + severe packet loss! Always verify with ethtool eth0.

2.3 MAC Addresses — The Hardware Identity

A MAC (Media Access Control) address is a 48-bit identifier burned into every network interface. It's the "physical address" used for L2 delivery within a local network segment.

🏷️
Format

aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff
First 24 bits = OUI (Organizationally Unique Identifier) — tells you the manufacturer
Last 24 bits = NIC-specific, assigned by manufacturer

🔍
Find MAC on Linux

ip link show | grep ether
ip link show eth0 → link/ether aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff

📨
Unicast

First byte is EVEN (bit 0 = 0)
Example: aa:bb:... (aa = 10100010)
"Send to ONE specific host"

📢
Broadcast

ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff
"Send to EVERYONE on this L2 segment"
ARP requests use broadcast

👥
Multicast

Starts with 01:00:5E
"Send to a GROUP of interested hosts"
Used by routing protocols (OSPF), streaming

⚠️
Key Fact

MAC addresses are L2-only — they never cross a router. If you see the same MAC on two different subnets, you're on the same broadcast domain.

2.4 Switches — The Silent Workhorses

A network switch is essentially a multi-port bridge that forwards Ethernet frames based on destination MAC addresses. Unlike hubs (which repeat to all ports), switches learn which MAC addresses are on which ports and forward intelligently.

How a Switch Learns — The 4-Step MAC Learning Process
  SWITCH MAC ADDRESS TABLE (initially empty)
  ┌──────────────────┬─────────┐
  │    MAC Address    │  Port   │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────┤
  │     (empty)      │         │
  └──────────────────┴─────────┘

  STEP 1: Host A (MAC: aa:bb:cc:11:22:33) on Port 1 → sends frame to Host B
  STEP 2: Switch LEARNS: aa:bb:cc:11:22:33 → Port 1 (records in CAM table)
  STEP 3: Switch doesn't know B's MAC → FLOODS frame to ALL ports (except Port 1)
  STEP 4: Host B responds → Switch LEARNS B's MAC → Port 2
  RESULT: Now A↔B traffic is FORWARDED only between Ports 1 and 2

  🎯 THE THREE F's:
  - FORWARD: Known MAC → send only to the correct port
  - FLOOD:   Unknown MAC → send to ALL ports (learning opportunity)
  - FILTER:  Same port as source → do NOT forward (already on that segment)

Managed vs Unmanaged Switches

FeatureUnmanagedManaged
Plug & Play✅ YesNeeds config
VLAN Support❌ No✅ 802.1Q
SNMP Monitoring❌ No✅ Yes
Port Mirroring (SPAN)❌ No✅ For tcpdump
Link Aggregation❌ No✅ LACP
Cost$20-50$200-5000+
⚠️ GOTCHA: VLAN Misconfigurations Are Silent Killers

A misconfigured VLAN tag means the switch drops the frame silently. No ICMP error, no log entry — the packet simply disappears. This is why VLAN issues are notoriously hard to debug. Always verify both ends have matching VLAN configurations.

2.5 ARP — How IP Addresses Become MAC Addresses

The Address Resolution Protocol (ARP) solves a fundamental problem: you know an IP address (192.168.1.5), but to deliver an Ethernet frame, you need the corresponding MAC address. ARP bridges Layer 3 (IP) and Layer 2 (MAC).

ARP Exchange — Annotated tcpdump Output
  # ARP REQUEST (Broadcast to ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff)
  10:23:45.123456 ARP, Request who-has 10.0.1.5 tell 10.0.1.1, length 28
  0x0000:  ffff ffff ffff  ← Destination MAC = Broadcast!
  0x0006:  aabb ccdd ee01  ← Source MAC = 10.0.1.1's interface
  0x000c:  0806            ← EtherType = 0x0806 (ARP)

  # ARP REPLY (Unicast back to requester)
  10:23:45.123458 ARP, Reply 10.0.1.5 is-at aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:05, length 28

ARP Request & Reply — The Full Exchange

ARP solves a fundamental problem: IP needs L2 delivery, but L2 only understands MAC addresses. Here's exactly how the exchange works, step by step:

ARP Exchange — Complete Packet Walk
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                         ARP REQUEST (Broadcast)                          │
  │                                                                          │
  │  Host A (10.0.1.1) wants to send to 10.0.1.5, but doesn't know its MAC │
  │                                                                          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  ETHERNET HEADER:                                                 │   │
  │  │  Dest MAC: ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff  ← BROADCAST! Everyone must read    │   │
  │  │  Src MAC:  aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:01  ← Host A's MAC                     │   │
  │  │  EtherType: 0x0806             ← This is an ARP packet            │   │
  │  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤   │
  │  │  ARP PAYLOAD:                                                     │   │
  │  │  Hardware Type: 1 (Ethernet)   Protocol Type: 0x0800 (IPv4)      │   │
  │  │  HW Addr Len: 6                Proto Addr Len: 4                  │   │
  │  │  Operation: 1 (REQUEST)                                           │   │
  │  │  Sender MAC:  aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:01  Sender IP:  10.0.1.1            │   │
  │  │  Target MAC:  00:00:00:00:00:00  ← UNKNOWN! This is what we ask  │   │
  │  │  Target IP:   10.0.1.5           ← "Who has this IP?"             │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        ARP REPLY (Unicast)                               │
  │                                                                          │
  │  Host B (10.0.1.5) recognizes its IP, responds directly to Host A       │
  │                                                                          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  ETHERNET HEADER:                                                 │   │
  │  │  Dest MAC: aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:01  ← UNICAST back to Host A only      │   │
  │  │  Src MAC:  aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:05  ← Host B's MAC                     │   │
  │  │  EtherType: 0x0806                                                │   │
  │  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤   │
  │  │  ARP PAYLOAD:                                                     │   │
  │  │  Operation: 2 (REPLY)                                             │   │
  │  │  Sender MAC:  aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:05  Sender IP:  10.0.1.5            │   │
  │  │  Target MAC:  aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:01  Target IP:   10.0.1.1            │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  RESULT: Host A caches 10.0.1.5 → aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:05 in its ARP table
          Subsequent packets skip ARP and use the cached MAC directly
📖 Why Broadcast for Request, Unicast for Reply?

The request MUST be broadcast because the sender doesn't know which MAC to send to — that's the whole point! But the reply can be unicast because the responder now knows the requester's MAC from the request packet. This is efficient: only one broadcast per new IP-to-MAC mapping.

# Watch ARP in action (run on any Linux machine)
$ sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -nn arp
tcpdump: verbose output suppressed, use -v or -vv

# ARP Request: "Who has 10.0.1.5?"
10:23:45.123456 ARP, Request who-has 10.0.1.5 tell 10.0.1.1, length 28

# ARP Reply: "10.0.1.5 is at aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:05"
10:23:45.123458 ARP, Reply 10.0.1.5 is-at aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:05, length 28

ARP Cache & States — The Full State Machine

Linux maintains an ARP cache to avoid broadcasting an ARP request for every single packet. Each entry transitions through states as it ages and gets verified:

ARP Cache State Machine — Complete Transition Diagram
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     ARP ENTRY LIFECYCLE                              │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌───────────┐    use & confirm     ┌───────────┐                   │
  │  │           │◄─────────────────────│           │                   │
  │  │ REACHABLE │                      │   STALE   │                   │
  │  │ (healthy) │─────────────────────▶│ (aged out)│                   │
  │  │           │  timer expires       │           │                   │
  │  └─────┬─────┘  (~30s default)      └─────┬─────┘                   │
  │        │                                   │                         │
  │        │ confirm fails                     │ first packet sent       │
  │        ▼                                   ▼                         │
  │  ┌───────────┐                      ┌───────────┐                   │
  │  │           │   timer expires      │           │                   │
  │  │  FAILED   │◄─────────────────────│   DELAY   │                   │
  │  │ (broken)  │   no response        │ (waiting) │                   │
  │  │           │                      │           │                   │
  │  └───────────┘                      └─────┬─────┘                   │
  │        │                                   │                         │
  │        │ garbage collected                 │ timer expires           │
  │        ▼                                   ▼                         │
  │     (deleted)                        ┌───────────┐                   │
  │                                      │           │                   │
  │                                      │   PROBE   │                   │
  │                                      │ (verify)  │                   │
  │                                      │           │                   │
  │                                      └─────┬─────┘                   │
  │                                            │                         │
  │                               confirm ┌───┴───┐ no confirm          │
  │                               ───────▶│       │────────▶ FAILED     │
  │                                       │ again │                    │
  │                                       └───────┘                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

# Check your ARP cache now
$ ip neigh show
10.0.1.1 dev eth0 lladdr aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff REACHABLE   ← Confirmed working
10.0.1.5 dev eth0 lladdr 11:22:33:44:55:66 STALE       ← Aged, still usable
10.0.1.10 dev eth0 lladdr 77:88:99:aa:bb:cc DELAY       ← First use after stale
10.0.1.20 dev eth0 lladdr dd:ee:ff:11:22:33 PROBE       ← Actively re-verifying
10.0.1.30 dev eth0 FAILED                                ← Unreachable!

# Key state meanings:
# REACHABLE = confirmed working (traffic flowing both ways)
# STALE     = not used recently, but cached MAC still trusted
# DELAY     = first packet sent to STALE entry; waiting briefly for TCP ACK
# PROBE     = actively sending ARP requests to re-verify
# FAILED    = no ARP reply after multiple probes → connection will fail
⚠️ GOTCHA: STALE ≠ Broken

ip neigh show showing STALE doesn't mean broken. STALE = "still usable, will verify on next use." Only FAILED means the neighbor is truly unreachable. Many engineers panic when they see STALE entries — don't be one of them!

Gratuitous ARP — Announcing Yourself Without Being Asked

A Gratuitous ARP (GARP) is an unsolicited ARP announcement — a host broadcasts its own IP-to-MAC mapping without anyone asking. This is critical for high availability and IP failover.

Gratuitous ARP in Action — keepalived/VRRP Failover
  BEFORE FAILOVER:
  ┌──────────────────┐         ┌──────────────────┐
  │   NODE-1 (MASTER) │         │  NODE-2 (BACKUP)  │
  │  Real IP: .10     │         │  Real IP: .20     │
  │  VIP: 10.0.1.100  │         │  (idle, watching) │
  │  MAC: aa:bb:...:01│         │  MAC: aa:bb:...:02│
  └────────┬─────────┘         └────────┬─────────┘
           │                            │
  ARP TABLE (all hosts):               │
  10.0.1.100 → aa:bb:...:01 (Node-1)   │
           │                            │
           │   💥 NODE-1 CRASHES!       │
           ▼                            ▼
  ┌──────────────────┐         ┌──────────────────┐
  │   NODE-1 (DEAD)   │         │  NODE-2 (MASTER) │
  │                   │         │  VIP: 10.0.1.100 │
  │                   │         │  MAC: aa:bb:...:02│
  └──────────────────┘         └────────┬─────────┘
                                        │
                     ┌──────────────────┘
                     ▼
  GARP BROADCAST: "10.0.1.100 is at aa:bb:...:02"
  (Node-2 announces it now owns the VIP)

  RESULT: All ARP caches updated INSTANTLY!
  10.0.1.100 → aa:bb:...:02 (Node-2)
  Downtime: <1 second (not 30-60s ARP timeout!)

  # Send a gratuitous ARP manually:
  $ sudo arping -A -I eth0 -c 3 10.0.1.100
  ARPING 10.0.1.100 from 10.0.1.100 eth0
  Sent 3 probes (3 broadcast(s))
  Received 0 response(s)  ← No response = no duplicate IP (good!)
💡 GARP in Kubernetes

When a Kubernetes Service of type LoadBalancer assigns an external IP, and that IP is managed by a controller like MetalLB, GARP is used to announce the IP's new location when pods move between nodes. Without GARP, external traffic would continue going to the old node until the ARP cache expires (30-60 seconds of downtime!).

ARP Spoofing & Security — Man-in-the-Middle Attack Explained

ARP spoofing (ARP poisoning) is one of the oldest and most effective LAN attacks. Because ARP has no authentication, any host can claim to be any IP address.

ARP Spoofing Attack — Step by Step
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                       NORMAL OPERATION                               │
  │                                                                      │
  │  Host A (10.0.1.5) ──────────▶ Gateway (10.0.1.1) ──────▶ INTERNET  │
  │  ARP: "Who has 10.0.1.1?"     MAC: gg:gg:gg:gg:gg:01                │
  │  Reply: "10.0.1.1 is at       Traffic flows normally                 │
  │          gg:gg:gg:gg:gg:01"                                           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     ARP SPOOFING ATTACK                               │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌─────────┐                                                        │
  │  │ ATTACKER │  Sends FORGED ARP replies:                             │
  │  │ 10.0.1.99│  "10.0.1.1 is at aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:99" (attacker's MAC) │
  │  └────┬────┘                                                        │
  │       │                                                              │
  │       ├──────────────────▶ Host A (poisoned)                         │
  │       │   Host A updates ARP cache WRONG:                            │
  │       │   10.0.1.1 → aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:99  (attacker, not gateway!)   │
  │       │                                                              │
  │       │   Host A sends traffic "to gateway" ────▶ ACTUALLY GOES TO   │
  │       │                                           ATTACKER!          │
  │       ▼                                                              │
  │  ┌─────────┐     Attacker reads/modifies traffic,                    │
  │  │ ATTACKER │     then forwards to REAL gateway                       │
  │  │ (MITM)   │────▶ Gateway ────▶ INTERNET                            │
  │  └─────────┘     Victim never knows!                                 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Defense Mechanisms — How to Protect Against ARP Spoofing

DefenseHow It WorksWhere
Static ARP EntriesManually configure critical IP→MAC mappings (e.g., gateway). ARP replies for these IPs are ignored.ip neigh add 10.0.1.1 lladdr gg:gg:... dev eth0 nud permanent
DHCP SnoopingSwitch builds a trusted IP→MAC→Port binding table from DHCP traffic. Drops ARP packets that don't match.Managed switch feature (Cisco, Juniper, Arista)
Dynamic ARP Inspection (DAI)Switch validates ARP packets against the DHCP snooping database. Invalid ARP → dropped + port can be shut down.Managed switch (Cisco: ip arp inspection vlan 10)
ARPwatch / arpalertMonitors ARP traffic and alerts on new or changed IP→MAC mappings. Doesn't prevent, but detects quickly.Linux host: apt install arpwatch
⚠️ Why ARP Spoofing Still Works in Cloud Environments

In cloud VPCs (AWS, GCP, Azure), ARP spoofing is mitigated at the hypervisor level — the cloud provider's SDN (Software-Defined Networking) maps IPs to instances and blocks spoofed ARP. But in bare-metal Kubernetes clusters or on-premise data centers, ARP spoofing is still a real threat. Network Policies in Kubernetes don't block ARP — they operate at L3/L4, while ARP is L2.

2.6 [LAB 🛠️] Layer 2 Hands-On with anihpj

Run these commands on your Kubernetes worker node or any Linux machine:

# 1. Inspect your interfaces
ip link show
ethtool eth0

# 2. Check your ARP cache
ip neigh show

# 3. Watch ARP in real time (run in one terminal)
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -nn arp

# 4. Find the MAC of your default gateway
ip route | grep default
ip neigh show | grep <gateway-ip>

# 5. K8s scenario: Debug pod-to-gateway ARP
kubectl exec -it anihpj-pod -- ip neigh show
kubectl exec -it anihpj-pod -- arping -I eth0 <gateway-ip>

📝 Chapter 2 — Networking Practice Questions

Q1
What are the 6 fields in an Ethernet frame and how many bytes does each occupy?
Show Answer
Answer
Preamble + SFD8 bytesSynchronization + frame delimiter
Destination MAC6 bytesWho should receive this
Source MAC6 bytesWho sent this
EtherType2 bytes0x0800=IPv4, 0x0806=ARP, 0x86DD=IPv6
Payload46-1500 bytesThe actual data (IP packet)
FCS (CRC32)4 bytesError detection checksum

Min frame: 64 bytes (8+6+6+2+46+4). Max: 1518 bytes (without VLAN tag).

Explanation

The minimum payload of 46 bytes exists because Ethernet originally needed 64 bytes total for collision detection (CSMA/CD on half-duplex). Smaller payloads are padded. Jumbo frames (MTU 9000) bypass this limit for high-throughput scenarios.

Q2
A Docker overlay network uses VXLAN. Your physical NIC has MTU 1500. Explain what happens when a container sends a 1500-byte packet and how to fix it.
Show Answer
Answer

VXLAN adds 50 bytes of overhead (outer Ethernet + IP + UDP + VXLAN headers). The total frame becomes 1550 bytes — exceeding the 1500 MTU. If DF (Don't Fragment) is set, the packet is dropped silently with no ICMP error (PMTU black hole). Solution: increase physical NIC MTU to 1550+ OR configure the container/pod network to use MTU 1450.

Explanation

This is a real production issue with Kubernetes CNI plugins like Flannel (VXLAN mode). Always check: ip link show | grep mtu on your nodes. Calico with eBPF avoids VXLAN entirely by using direct routing.

Q3
What type of MAC address is 01:00:5E:01:02:03 and what is it used for?
Show Answer
Answer

It's a multicast MAC address. The 01:00:5E prefix maps to IPv4 multicast addresses (224.0.0.0/4). The lower 23 bits of the IP multicast address are mapped to the lower 23 bits of the MAC. Used by OSPF (224.0.0.5/6), VRRP, streaming protocols, etc.

Explanation

Broadcast (ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff) goes to everyone on the segment. Multicast goes only to hosts that have subscribed to that group — much more efficient for protocols like OSPF that don't need to bother every host.

Q4
You run ip neigh show and see: 10.0.1.1 dev eth0 lladdr aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff STALE. Is this broken? Explain.
Show Answer
Answer

No, STALE is not broken. STALE means the ARP entry hasn't been used recently but is still considered valid. On the next packet to 10.0.1.1, the kernel will use this entry and then move it to REACHABLE. Only entries in FAILED state are truly unreachable.

Explanation

ARP states: REACHABLE → STALE (timer expiry) → DELAY (first use after stale) → PROBE (actively arping) → REACHABLE or FAILED. This is the kernel's way of balancing performance (cached entries) with correctness (verifying stale entries).

Q5
What are the "Three F's" of switch operation? Explain each.
Show Answer
Answer

FORWARD: Destination MAC is in the CAM table → send frame only to the specific port.
FLOOD: Destination MAC is unknown → send frame to ALL ports (except source).
FILTER: Destination MAC is on the same port as source → do NOT forward (already on that segment).

Explanation

These three operations are the core of L2 switching. Flooding for unknown MACs is how the switch learns — the destination will eventually respond, and its MAC gets added to the CAM table. Filtering prevents unnecessary traffic on already-saturated segments.

Q6
What is a VLAN (802.1Q) and why would you use one? How does it affect broadcast domains?
Show Answer
Answer

A VLAN (Virtual LAN) splits one physical switch into multiple logical switches using 802.1Q tags in the Ethernet header. Each VLAN is a separate broadcast domain — a broadcast on VLAN 10 does NOT reach VLAN 20. This provides network segmentation without needing separate physical switches. Trunk ports carry multiple VLANs; access ports carry only one.

# Linux VLAN interface creation
ip link add link eth0 name eth0.10 type vlan id 10
ip addr add 10.0.10.1/24 dev eth0.10
ip link set eth0.10 up
Explanation

VLANs are fundamental to network segmentation. In Kubernetes, some CNI plugins use VLANs for pod network isolation. The 802.1Q tag adds 4 bytes to the Ethernet frame, increasing the minimum frame size to 68 bytes.

Q7
What is the difference between unicast, broadcast, and multicast MAC addresses? Give an example MAC for each.
Show Answer
Answer

Unicast: First byte is EVEN (bit 0 = 0). Example: aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff. Sent to ONE specific host.
Broadcast: ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff. Sent to ALL hosts on the L2 segment. Used by ARP requests, DHCP discover.
Multicast: Starts with 01:00:5E (IPv4) or 33:33 (IPv6). Sent to a GROUP of subscribed hosts. Used by OSPF, streaming, VRRP.

Explanation

The first byte's least significant bit determines unicast (0) vs multicast (1). Broadcast is a special case of multicast (all bits set). Switches treat these differently — unicast is forwarded to one port, broadcast/multicast are flooded to all ports (unless IGMP snooping is enabled for multicast).

Q8
In your Kubernetes cluster, a pod on node-1 can't reach a pod on node-2. Both pods have IPs in the 10.244.0.0/16 range. Walk through the L2 debugging steps on node-1.
Show Answer
Answer
# 1. Check pod's network interface is up (L1)
crictl inspect <pod-container-id> | jq .info.pid
nsenter -t <pid> -n ip link show eth0

# 2. Check ARP for the destination pod's node IP (L2)
nsenter -t <pid> -n ip neigh show | grep <node-2-ip>

# 3. Check if the CNI bridge (cni0) is forwarding
bridge fdb show br cni0

# 4. Check if VXLAN/VLAN encapsulation is working
ip -d link show vxlan.calico  # or flannel.1 for Flannel
Explanation

Cross-node pod communication in Kubernetes typically involves overlay networking (VXLAN) or direct routing, depending on the CNI plugin. The L2 path is: pod-eth0 → veth pair → node bridge (cni0) → VXLAN tunnel → physical NIC → network → other node → VXLAN decap → bridge → destination pod.

Q9
Explain gratuitous ARP (GARP). When is it used and why is it important for high availability?
Show Answer
Answer

A gratuitous ARP is an unsolicited ARP announcement — a host broadcasts "I am IP X at MAC Y" without being asked. It's used for: (1) IP takeover during failover (keepalived/VRRP moves a floating IP to a backup node), (2) duplicate IP detection (if another host responds, there's a conflict), (3) updating ARP caches after a MAC address change.

# Send a gratuitous ARP manually
arping -A -I eth0 10.0.1.100
Explanation

In Kubernetes, when a Service's ClusterIP fails over or a LoadBalancer IP moves between nodes, GARP ensures all switches and hosts update their ARP caches immediately — otherwise traffic would continue going to the old (dead) MAC for up to the ARP cache timeout (typically 30-60 seconds).

Q10
You suspect a bad Ethernet cable is causing intermittent packet loss on your Kubernetes node. What commands would you run to diagnose this at Layer 1?
Show Answer
Answer
# Check link state and speed
ethtool eth0           # Speed, Duplex, Link detected
ip link show eth0      # UP/DOWN state, MTU

# Check error counters (THE most important)
ip -s link show eth0   # RX errors, dropped, overruns, frame errors
ethtool -S eth0        # Driver-level statistics

# Check for CRC errors (physical layer issues)
# RX errors + frame errors = likely cable/duplex issue
# RX dropped = likely ring buffer too small
# RX overruns = NIC can't keep up with incoming traffic
Explanation

A "link up" status doesn't mean the link is healthy. ip -s link show exposes the real story through error counters. Increasing RX errors + frame errors typically indicates a bad cable or duplex mismatch. This is the first thing to check when pods on a specific node experience unexplained network issues.

Chapter 3: Wireshark & Packet Analysis Fundamentals

Packet analysis is an engineering superpower. When application logs say "connection timeout," only the packets can tell you WHY — was it a TCP RST? A dropped SYN? A firewall silently discarding traffic? This chapter teaches you to use tcpdump (CLI) and Wireshark (GUI) to see exactly what's happening on your network.

🟢 Beginner⏱️ ~1 hour🔬 Packet Analysis🛠️ Lab

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Why packets don't lie — and why application logs sometimes do
  • ✅ tcpdump — the essential CLI packet sniffer with BPF filters
  • ✅ Wireshark — the GUI that makes protocol analysis visual
  • ✅ How to capture packets on containers and Kubernetes pods

3.1 Why Packet Analysis Is an Engineering Superpower

Logs say "connection timeout." But WHY? Was it a TCP RST from the server? A SYN that never got a response? A firewall silently dropping packets? Only packet analysis answers these questions definitively. As the saying goes: "The packets don't lie."

Capture Tool Decision Tree — Which Tool for Which Scenario
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WHERE ARE YOU CAPTURING?                                    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                                              │
  │  🖥️ Local Linux server     → tcpdump -i eth0 port 8000       │
  │  📦 Docker container       → nsenter -t <pid> -n tcpdump    │
  │  ☸️ Kubernetes pod         → kubectl debug or kubectl exec   │
  │  🪟 Windows desktop        → Wireshark (GUI)                 │
  │  🌐 Remote server          → tcpdump + scp the .pcap file    │
  │  📊 Deep protocol analysis → Wireshark (open .pcap file)    │
  │  🤖 Scripted analysis      → tshark -r capture.pcap -Y ...  │
  │                                                              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
⚠️ GOTCHA: Always Use Filters

Running tcpdump -i eth0 without a BPF filter on a busy server can overwhelm the CPU. BPF runs in the kernel (efficient), but output to stdout is expensive. Always use filters: tcpdump -i eth0 port 8000 and host 10.0.1.5.

3.2 tcpdump — The Command-Line Packet Sniffer

tcpdump is the universal packet capture tool — available on every Linux distribution, container image, and even inside Kubernetes pods. It uses BPF (Berkeley Packet Filter) expressions to filter packets at the kernel level before they reach userspace.

# Basic syntax
tcpdump -i <interface> [BPF filter expression]

# Essential filters
tcpdump -i eth0 host 10.0.1.5          # Only traffic to/from 10.0.1.5
tcpdump -i eth0 port 8000              # Only port 8000 traffic
tcpdump -i eth0 net 10.0.1.0/24       # Only subnet traffic
tcpdump -i eth0 tcp                    # Only TCP
tcpdump -i eth0 'tcp and port 8000'   # TCP + port 8000
tcpdump -i eth0 'src host 10.0.1.5'   # FROM 10.0.1.5 only
tcpdump -i eth0 'dst port 443'        # TO port 443 only

# Save to file, read back
tcpdump -i eth0 -w capture.pcap        # Save raw packets
tcpdump -r capture.pcap                # Read pcap file
tcpdump -r capture.pcap 'port 8000'    # Filter on read

# Production-safe ring buffer
tcpdump -i eth0 -W 5 -C 100 -Z root   # 5 files × 100MB = 500MB max
⚠️ GOTCHA: Default Snap Length

tcpdump by default only captures the first 96 bytes of each packet. Use -s 0 for full packet capture. Without -s 0, you'll miss HTTP bodies, long headers, and application payload data.

Reading tcpdump Output — Line by Line

Understanding tcpdump's output format is essential for quick debugging. Here's how to decode every field in a typical packet capture line:

tcpdump Output — Annotated Breakdown
  ┌────────── Timestamp ──────────┐  ┌── Src ──┐ ┌── Dst ──┐
  │                                │  │         │ │         │
  14:23:45.123456 IP 10.244.1.5.45678 > 10.244.2.10.8000:
                                               │            │
                                               └── Src Port └── Dst Port
  Flags [S], seq 123456789, win 65535,
  ↑                    ↑            ↑
  TCP Flags        Sequence #    Window Size
  options [mss 1460,sackOK,TS val 12345 ecr 0,nop,wscale 7], length 0
           ↑                                                          ↑
        TCP Options                                             Payload size

  COMMON FLAGS:
  [S] = SYN (connection start)      [S.] = SYN-ACK
  [.] = ACK (acknowledgment)        [P.] = PUSH (send data now)
  [F.] = FIN (graceful close)       [R.] = RST (abrupt reset)

  TCP HANDSHAKE EXAMPLE:
  1. 14:23:45.100 IP 10.1.5.45678 > 10.2.10.8000: Flags [S]        SYN
  2. 14:23:45.101 IP 10.2.10.8000 > 10.1.5.45678: Flags [S.]       SYN-ACK
  3. 14:23:45.101 IP 10.1.5.45678 > 10.2.10.8000: Flags [.]        ACK
  → Connection ESTABLISHED!

  HTTP REQUEST/RESPONSE:
  4. 14:23:45.102 IP 10.1.5.45678 > 10.2.10.8000: Flags [P.],      HTTP GET
     length 150: HTTP: GET /api/jobs/ HTTP/1.1
  5. 14:23:45.105 IP 10.2.10.8000 > 10.1.5.45678: Flags [P.],      HTTP 200
     length 450: HTTP: HTTP/1.1 200 OK

20 Essential tcpdump Filter Recipes

Scenariotcpdump Command
HTTP traffic to anihpjtcpdump -i eth0 'tcp port 8000'
DNS queries onlytcpdump -i eth0 'udp port 53'
All traffic from a specific pod IPtcpdump -i eth0 'host 10.244.1.5'
ARP packets onlytcpdump -i eth0 arp
ICMP (ping) onlytcpdump -i eth0 icmp
SYN packets (new connections)tcpdump -i eth0 'tcp[tcpflags] & (tcp-syn) != 0'
RST packets (connection resets)tcpdump -i eth0 'tcp[tcpflags] & (tcp-rst) != 0'
Traffic between two podstcpdump -i eth0 'host 10.244.1.5 and host 10.244.2.10'
Traffic NOT from SSHtcpdump -i eth0 'not port 22'
HTTP POST requeststcpdump -i eth0 -A 'tcp port 8000' | grep POST

3.3 Wireshark — The GUI That Makes Packets Visible

Wireshark is the gold standard for deep protocol analysis. Its three-pane interface and powerful display filters let you dissect protocols at every layer.

📋
Packet List (Top)

Summary of each captured packet: number, timestamp, source, destination, protocol, length, info. Click to select.

🌳
Packet Details (Middle)

Tree view of the selected packet's protocol layers — expand Ethernet → IP → TCP → HTTP. Every field decoded.

🔢
Packet Bytes (Bottom)

Raw hex dump + ASCII representation. Click a field in the tree and the corresponding bytes are highlighted.

🔍
Display Filters

Post-capture filtering: http, tcp.port==8000, dns.qry.name contains "anihpj". Much more powerful than BPF.

🎨
Coloring Rules

TCP RST = red, TCP SYN = dark gray, HTTP = green, DNS = blue. Instantly spot anomalies by color.

📊
Statistics Menu

I/O Graph (throughput over time), TCP Stream Graph (seq/ack analysis), Protocol Hierarchy, Conversations.

3.4 [LAB 🛠️] Packet Capture Lab

# 1. Capture a curl request to the anihpj API
sudo tcpdump -i any -w anihpj.pcap port 8000 &
curl http://localhost:8000/api/jobs/
sudo pkill tcpdump

# 2. Read back and filter for just HTTP GET
tcpdump -r anihpj.pcap 'tcp[((tcp[12:1] & 0xf0) >> 2):4] = 0x47455420'

# 3. Follow the TCP stream in Wireshark
# Open anihpj.pcap → Right-click any packet → Follow → TCP Stream

# 4. Identify the key packets:
# SYN (flags [S]), SYN-ACK ([S.]), ACK ([.]),
# HTTP GET, HTTP/1.1 200 OK, FIN ([F.]), FIN-ACK, ACK

# 5. From inside a Kubernetes pod:
kubectl exec -it anihpj-pod -- tcpdump -i eth0 -w /tmp/capture.pcap
kubectl cp anihpj-pod:/tmp/capture.pcap ./pod-capture.pcap

📝 Chapter 3 — Networking Practice Questions

Q1
What is the difference between a capture filter (BPF) and a display filter in Wireshark?
Show Answer
Answer

Capture filters (BPF): Applied BEFORE capture. Uses Berkeley Packet Filter syntax. Runs in kernel — very efficient. Limits what packets are captured. Example: tcp port 8000.
Display filters: Applied AFTER capture. Uses Wireshark's own syntax. Runs in userspace. Filters what you SEE from already-captured packets. Example: http.request.method == "GET".

Explanation

Use capture filters when you know exactly what traffic you need (saves disk space, CPU). Use display filters when you want to capture everything and explore different views later.

Q2
You need to capture HTTP traffic to/from anihpj running on port 8000 inside a Kubernetes pod. Write the commands.
Show Answer
Answer
# Option 1: kubectl exec with tcpdump installed in the image
kubectl exec anihpj-pod -- tcpdump -i eth0 -w /tmp/cap.pcap port 8000
kubectl cp anihpj-pod:/tmp/cap.pcap ./anihpj.pcap

# Option 2: kubectl debug (ephemeral container)
kubectl debug -it anihpj-pod --image=nicolaka/netshoot -- tcpdump -i eth0 port 8000

# Option 3: From the host (find the pod's network namespace)
POD_PID=$(crictl inspect <container-id> | jq .info.pid)
nsenter -t $POD_PID -n tcpdump -i eth0 port 8000
Explanation

kubectl debug is the cleanest method — it adds a temporary sidecar container with networking tools. The nsenter approach works from the Kubernetes node directly and doesn't require modifying the pod.

Q3
Why does tcpdump default to capturing only 96 bytes per packet? What flag overrides this and when should you use it?
Show Answer
Answer

96 bytes (historically called "snaplen") captures Ethernet header (14) + IP header (20) + TCP header (20-60) = enough for protocol analysis while saving disk space and CPU. Use -s 0 for full packet capture. Essential when: debugging HTTP body content, TLS certificate data, DNS response payloads, or any application-layer data.

Explanation

For production captures with ring buffers (-W 5 -C 100), you typically DON'T want -s 0 because the files would be enormous. Use -s 0 only for targeted debugging sessions.

Q4
What is the difference between Wireshark's capture filter and display filter? Write an example of each.
Show Answer
Answer

Capture filter (BPF): Applied before capture, runs in kernel, limits WHAT is captured. Example: tcp port 8000 and host 10.0.1.5. Uses BPF syntax.
Display filter: Applied after capture, runs in userspace, filters what you SEE from already-captured data. Example: http.request.method == "GET" and ip.src == 10.0.1.5. Uses Wireshark display filter syntax.

Explanation

Key difference: capture filters are irreversible — packets filtered out are gone forever. Display filters are non-destructive — you can change/remove them and see all captured packets again. Use capture filters to limit file size; use display filters to explore different views of the same data.

Q5
You need to debug TLS connection issues to anihpj.com. Write the tcpdump and Wireshark steps to capture and analyze the TLS handshake.
Show Answer
Answer
# Capture TLS traffic (port 443)
sudo tcpdump -i any -w tls.pcap port 443

# In Wireshark:
# 1. Open tls.pcap
# 2. Display filter: tls.handshake.type
# 3. Look for: Client Hello → Server Hello → Certificate → Server Hello Done
# 4. Check for TLS alerts (tls.alert_message.level) for errors
# 5. Statistics → TLS → see cipher suites, certificates, handshake times

# CLI alternative with tshark:
tshark -r tls.pcap -Y "tls.handshake.type == 1"  # Client Hello
tshark -r tls.pcap -Y "tls.handshake.type == 2"  # Server Hello
tshark -r tls.pcap -Y "tls.alert_message.level"   # TLS errors
Explanation

TLS debugging with packet analysis is powerful because you can see exactly which step of the handshake fails — e.g., if the server sends a Certificate that the client rejects, or if the cipher suite negotiation fails. This is much more informative than openssl error messages alone.

Q6
What BPF filter would you use to capture only HTTP GET requests to port 8000? Explain the filter syntax.
Show Answer
Answer
# Capture TCP traffic to port 8000
tcpdump -i eth0 'tcp port 8000'

# More specific: only packets with TCP payload (SYN/ACK without data excluded)
tcpdump -i eth0 'tcp port 8000 and greater 100'

# Filter structure:
# 'tcp'           = protocol filter
# 'port 8000'     = port filter (src or dst)
# 'greater 100'   = packet length > 100 bytes (excludes empty ACKs)
# You can combine with: and, or, not
Explanation

BPF (Berkeley Packet Filter) expressions are compiled to bytecode that runs in the kernel — making them extremely efficient. You can see the compiled bytecode with tcpdump -d 'tcp port 8000'. This is the same filtering engine used by Wireshark, libpcap, and most packet capture tools.

Q7
How would you capture packets from a specific Kubernetes pod that doesn't have tcpdump installed? Provide three different methods.
Show Answer
Answer
# Method 1: kubectl debug (ephemeral container)
kubectl debug -it anihpj-pod --image=nicolaka/netshoot \
  --target=anihpj -- tcpdump -i eth0 port 8000

# Method 2: From the host node via nsenter
POD_PID=$(crictl inspect <container-id> | jq -r '.info.pid')
sudo nsenter -t $POD_PID -n tcpdump -i eth0 port 8000

# Method 3: kubectl exec with tcpdump binary (if available)
kubectl cp /usr/sbin/tcpdump anihpj-pod:/tmp/tcpdump
kubectl exec anihpj-pod -- /tmp/tcpdump -i eth0 port 8000
Explanation

Method 1 (kubectl debug) is the cleanest — it adds a temporary sidecar container sharing the pod's network namespace. Method 2 (nsenter) is the "SRE power tool" — it works from the node level without touching the pod. Method 3 is a hack but works when kubectl debug isn't available (older K8s versions).

Q8
In Wireshark, you see a packet colored red. What does this typically indicate? Name three common Wireshark coloring rules.
Show Answer
Answer

Red typically indicates a TCP RST (Reset) packet — an abnormal connection termination. Common coloring rules: (1) Red: TCP RST — connection forcefully closed, (2) Dark Gray: TCP SYN — new connection attempt, (3) Green: HTTP traffic — web data, (4) Light Blue: DNS — domain name queries/responses, (5) Yellow: TCP retransmissions — potential network issues.

Explanation

Coloring rules are Wireshark's way of helping you spot patterns instantly. A flood of red (RST) packets might indicate a firewall blocking connections. Yellow retransmissions suggest packet loss. These visual cues save you from reading every packet header manually.

Q9
What is the purpose of tcpdump -d? Show what it outputs and explain its significance.
Show Answer
Answer
$ tcpdump -d 'tcp port 8000'
(000) ldh      [12]
(001) jeq      #0x86dd          jt 2    jf 7
(002) ldb      [20]
(003) jeq      #0x6             jt 10   jf 4
...
(010) ldh      [54]
(011) jeq      #0x1f40          jt 16   jf 12
...

This is the compiled BPF bytecode that the kernel executes for every packet. It shows exactly how your filter is translated into efficient kernel-level instructions.

Explanation

BPF bytecode is a mini virtual machine that runs inside the kernel. Each instruction is simple (load, jump, compare). This is why BPF filters are so fast — there's no context switching, no userspace processing. eBPF (extended BPF) takes this concept to the next level, allowing programmable networking in the kernel (used by Cilium).

Q10
You're analyzing a pcap file from a failed HTTP request to anihpj. You see: SYN → SYN-ACK → ACK → HTTP GET → ... then nothing. The client eventually sends RST. What layer is the problem at and what tools would you use next?
Show Answer
Answer

The TCP connection was established successfully (SYN→SYN-ACK→ACK) and the HTTP request was sent, but no HTTP response was received. This indicates a Layer 7 (Application) problem — the web server received the request but didn't respond. Next steps: (1) Check application logs (kubectl logs anihpj-pod), (2) Check if the application process is hung (CPU/memory), (3) Check if the database query is timing out, (4) Try curl -v directly to see the full HTTP exchange.

Explanation

The packet capture tells you exactly what happened and where: L1-L4 are fine (connection established), L7 is the problem (no response). This narrows your debugging from "the site is down" to "the application is not responding to requests" — saving potentially hours of checking network infrastructure that's actually working fine.

Chapter 4: Linux Network Stack — What Happens Inside the Kernel

Every packet that enters or leaves your Linux machine travels through a precise path in the kernel. Understanding this path — from NIC driver → netfilter → routing → socket → application — is essential for debugging network issues, writing firewall rules, and understanding how containers isolate networking.

🟢 Beginner⏱️ ~1.5 hours🐧 Linux Kernel🔧 Hands-On

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The Linux network stack architecture — from NIC to application socket
  • ✅ Network interfaces — physical, virtual, veth pairs, and bridges
  • ✅ The ip command — your Swiss Army knife for network management
  • ✅ Network namespaces — the magic behind Docker and Kubernetes isolation
  • ✅ Linux bridges — software switches that connect containers
  • ✅ /proc/net/ — raw kernel networking state you can inspect

4.1 The Linux Network Stack Architecture

When your anihpj Django app receives an HTTP request, the packet travels through this exact path in the Linux kernel:

Linux Kernel Network Stack — Complete Packet Walk
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                         USER SPACE                                │
  │  ┌─────────┐  ┌─────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────────┐       │
  │  │  curl   │  │  nginx  │  │ gunicorn │  │  anihpj app  │       │
  │  └────┬────┘  └────┬────┘  └────┬─────┘  └──────┬───────┘       │
  │       │             │            │                │               │
  │  ─────┼─────────────┼────────────┼────────────────┼── SOCKET API │
  │       │   socket()  │  accept()  │    recv()      │              │
  │  ─────┼─────────────┼────────────┼────────────────┼──            │
  │       ▼             ▼            ▼                ▼               │
  │  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗        │
  │  ║            TCP/UDP PROTOCOL STACK                    ║ KERNEL │
  │  ║  (connection tracking, retransmission, buffering)    ║ SPACE  │
  │  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣        │
  │  ║            IP LAYER — ROUTING DECISION               ║        │
  │  ║  (routing table lookup → which interface?)           ║        │
  │  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣        │
  │  ║            NETFILTER HOOKS (iptables/nftables)       ║        │
  │  ║  PREROUTING → INPUT/FORWARD → POSTROUTING (NAT)     ║        │
  │  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣        │
  │  ║            ETHERNET / ARP LAYER                      ║        │
  │  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣        │
  │  ║            NIC DRIVER → DMA → HARDWARE               ║        │
  │  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Connection Tracking (conntrack) — The Kernel's Memory of Connections

Linux maintains a connection tracking table that records every active network connection. This is how the kernel knows that a response packet belongs to an existing connection — essential for NAT (Network Address Translation) and stateful firewalls. Without conntrack, the kernel couldn't match return traffic to outgoing connections.

# View all tracked connections
conntrack -L
# tcp  6 431999 ESTABLISHED src=10.244.1.5 dst=10.244.2.10
#       sport=5432 dport=45678 [ASSURED] mark=0 use=1

# Key fields:
# - Protocol (6=TCP, 17=UDP)
# - Timeout (seconds remaining)
# - State (ESTABLISHED, TIME_WAIT, CLOSE, etc.)
# - Source/Destination IP and port
# - [ASSURED] = traffic seen in BOTH directions (reliable)
# - [UNREPLIED] = only one-way traffic seen

# Watch connections in real-time
conntrack -E
# See NEW connections being created, DESTROY events

# How many connections are tracked right now?
conntrack -C
# If this number approaches conntrack_max, NEW CONNECTIONS WILL BE DROPPED!
⚠️ GOTCHA: conntrack Table Full = Silent Connection Drops

When the conntrack table reaches net.netfilter.nf_conntrack_max, new connections are silently dropped. This is a classic production issue on high-traffic Kubernetes nodes. Check: conntrack -C (current count) vs sysctl net.netfilter.nf_conntrack_max (max). Symptoms: intermittent connection failures, DNS timeouts, service-to-service communication breaking randomly.

How the Routing Decision Works

After a packet passes through netfilter PREROUTING, the kernel makes a routing decision: is this packet for the local host, or should it be forwarded? This decision is based on the routing table and the packet's destination IP.

# View the routing table
ip route show
# default via 10.0.1.1 dev eth0
# 10.0.1.0/24 dev eth0 proto kernel scope link src 10.0.1.5
# 10.244.0.0/16 dev cni0 proto kernel scope link src 10.244.0.1

# Check which route a packet would take
ip route get 10.244.2.10
# 10.244.2.10 dev cni0 src 10.244.0.1

# The kernel's decision tree:
# 1. Is dst IP on a local interface? → LOCAL DELIVERY (socket)
# 2. Is there a specific route? → FORWARD via that route
# 3. Is there a default gateway? → FORWARD via default
# 4. None of the above? → DROP + "Network unreachable"

4.2 Network Interfaces in Linux

InterfaceTypeExamplePurpose
eth0, ens3PhysicalNIC portsConnects to physical network
loLoopback127.0.0.1Local communication within the host
docker0BridgeDocker defaultSoftware switch for Docker containers
cni0BridgeK8s defaultSoftware switch for Kubernetes pods
vethXXXXveth pairContainer linkVirtual cable connecting namespace to bridge
tun0 / wg0TunnelVPN interfaceEncrypted tunnel (WireGuard, OpenVPN)

4.3 The ip Command — Your Network Swiss Army Knife

The ip command from iproute2 has replaced the legacy tools (ifconfig, route, arp). Every modern Linux system uses it.

ip link — Interface Management

Manage the physical and virtual network interfaces on your Linux system. This is Layer 2 — interfaces, MAC addresses, MTU, and link state.

ip link show                    # List ALL interfaces (even down ones)
ip link show eth0               # Show specific interface
ip link set eth0 up             # Bring interface UP (enable)
ip link set eth0 down           # Bring interface DOWN (disable)
ip link set eth0 mtu 9000       # Set MTU for jumbo frames
ip link set eth0 address aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff  # Change MAC address!

# Real-world: Check if Docker bridge exists
ip link show docker0
# Real-world: Check pod interface inside K8s node
ip link show | grep veth       # All container veth pairs

Pro tip: ip link show output shows the interface index (first number), state (UP/DOWN/UNKNOWN), MTU, and MAC address. The NO-CARRIER flag means no physical cable connected.

ip addr — IP Address Management

Assign and manage IP addresses on interfaces. This bridges Layer 2 (the interface) with Layer 3 (the IP address).

ip addr show                    # Show all IP addresses + interface info
ip addr show eth0               # Show IPs on specific interface
ip addr add 10.0.1.5/24 dev eth0  # Add primary IP
ip addr add 10.0.1.6/24 dev eth0  # Add SECONDARY IP (yes, multiple!)
ip addr del 10.0.1.5/24 dev eth0  # Remove IP address

# Real-world: Check what IPs your K8s node has
ip addr show | grep "inet "     # Filter for just IPv4 addresses
# Real-world: Check pod IP from node
ip addr show | grep "10.244"    # K8s pod CIDR

Pro tip: One interface can have MULTIPLE IP addresses. This is called IP aliasing and is used for hosting multiple services on different IPs on the same NIC.

ip route — Routing Table

View and manage the kernel's routing table — the decision engine that determines which interface and next-hop each packet should use.

ip route show                   # Show main routing table
ip route show table all         # Show ALL routing tables
ip route add 10.0.2.0/24 via 10.0.1.1    # Add static route
ip route add default via 10.0.1.1        # Set default gateway
ip route del 10.0.2.0/24                 # Remove route
ip route get 8.8.8.8                     # SHOW what route kernel WOULD use

# Real-world: Check how K8s routes pod traffic
ip route show | grep 10.244     # Pod network routes
# Real-world: Verify default gateway
ip route show default           # Shows where non-local traffic goes

Pro tip: ip route get <dst> is invaluable — it shows you exactly which interface and source IP the kernel would use, factoring in all routing rules, not just the main routing table.

ip neigh — ARP Cache

Manage the ARP/neighbor cache — the L2-to-L3 mapping table that tracks which MAC address corresponds to which IP on the local network.

ip neigh show                   # Show all ARP entries with states
ip neigh show dev eth0          # Show ARP for specific interface
ip neigh add 10.0.1.5 lladdr aa:bb:... dev eth0  # Add static ARP
ip neigh del 10.0.1.5 dev eth0  # Remove ARP entry
ip neigh flush dev eth0         # Clear ALL ARP entries on interface
ip neigh replace 10.0.1.5 lladdr bb:cc:... dev eth0  # Update entry

# Real-world: Check if gateway ARP is healthy
ip neigh show | grep $(ip route show default | awk '{print $3}')
# Real-world: Flush ARP after a failover
ip neigh flush dev eth0; ping -c 1 10.0.1.1  # Force fresh ARP

Pro tip: After a keepalived/VRRP failover where a VIP moves to a new node, you often need to flush the ARP cache or send a GARP — otherwise traffic goes to the old (now dead) MAC for up to 30 seconds.

ip netns — Network Namespaces

Create and manage isolated network stacks. Each namespace has its own interfaces, routing table, ARP cache, and firewall rules — completely independent from the host and other namespaces.

ip netns add myns               # Create a new network namespace
ip netns list                    # List named namespaces (NOT Docker's!)
ip netns exec myns ip link      # Run command INSIDE the namespace
ip netns exec myns bash          # Get a shell inside the namespace
ip netns delete myns             # Delete namespace (must be unused)

# Move an interface INTO a namespace
ip link set veth1 netns myns    # veth1 now only visible inside myns

# Real-world: Enter a Docker container's netns from the host
PID=$(docker inspect -f '{{.State.Pid}}' container-name)
nsenter -t $PID -n ip link      # See container's interfaces from host
# Real-world: Debug K8s pod networking from the node
crictl inspect <container-id> | jq .info.pid
nsenter -t <pid> -n ip addr     # See pod's IP addresses

Pro tip: ip netns list only shows named namespaces (those created with ip netns add). Docker containers use anonymous namespaces that don't appear in this list. Use lsns -t net to see ALL network namespaces on the system.

4.4 Network Namespaces — The Magic Behind Containers
