The key pattern: COPY requirements.txt FIRST (before code). Docker caches layers — if requirements.txt hasn't changed, the pip install layer is reused. This makes rebuilds after code changes nearly instant (seconds vs minutes).

Q2
Why should Django apps bind to 0.0.0.0 in containers, not 127.0.0.1? Explain with packet flow.
Show Answer
Answer

127.0.0.1 is the loopback interface — only accessible FROM INSIDE the container. When Docker port-maps or routes traffic to the container, it arrives on the eth0 interface (from the veth pair). A process listening on 127.0.0.1:8000 never sees that traffic — it's on the wrong interface. 0.0.0.0 means "listen on ALL interfaces" — loopback, eth0, everything. Traffic from the Docker bridge, other containers, and port mapping all reach the process. gunicorn: --bind 0.0.0.0:8000. Django runserver: 0.0.0.0:8000.

Explanation

This is the #1 Docker networking gotcha for beginners. The container has its own network stack — 127.0.0.1 means the container's loopback, not the host's. Docker's port mapping DNATs traffic to the container's eth0 IP, which 127.0.0.1-bound processes never see.

Q3
What's the difference between CMD exec form and shell form? Why does it matter for networking?
Show Answer
Answer

Exec form: CMD ["gunicorn", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000", "myapp.wsgi"] — gunicorn runs as PID 1, receives SIGTERM directly, shuts down gracefully (finishes active requests). Shell form: CMD gunicorn --bind 0.0.0.0:8000 myapp.wsgi — a shell (/bin/sh) runs as PID 1, gunicorn is a child. The shell doesn't forward signals — when Docker stops the container, gunicorn gets SIGKILL after a timeout, dropping active connections. Networking impact: Shell form causes connection resets on shutdown; exec form allows graceful connection draining.

Explanation

In Kubernetes, this matters even more. Pod termination sends SIGTERM → wait gracePeriodSeconds → SIGKILL. With exec form, gunicorn can drain connections during the grace period. With shell form, the grace period is wasted because the signal never reaches gunicorn.

Q4
What does HEALTHCHECK do in a Dockerfile? How does Kubernetes use it?
Show Answer
Answer

Docker HEALTHCHECK: Runs a command inside the container periodically. If it returns 0, the container is "healthy." If it fails N times (retries), the container is "unhealthy." Docker shows status in docker ps. Kubernetes: Uses livenessProbe and readinessProbe (separate concepts) — HEALTHCHECK is NOT used by default in K8s. However, if no probes are defined, some K8s setups may use the Docker HEALTHCHECK as a fallback. Best practice: define explicit Kubernetes probes in your pod spec, don't rely on Docker HEALTHCHECK.

Explanation

Kubernetes probes are more powerful: livenessProbe (restart if stuck), readinessProbe (stop sending traffic if not ready), startupProbe (give slow-starting apps time). Docker HEALTHCHECK is simpler but K8s probes are the production standard.

Q5
What files should be in .dockerignore for a Django project? Why does it matter for networking security?
Show Answer
Answer

Must exclude: .env (contains SECRET_KEY, DB_PASSWORD — secrets in images are a MAJOR security risk), db.sqlite3 (local dev database — shouldn't be in the image), __pycache__/, *.pyc (compiled Python, not needed), .git/ (repository history, can contain secrets), env/, venv/ (virtual environments), media/, staticfiles/ (generated at build/runtime). Networking security: If .env leaks into the image and the image is pushed to a registry, anyone with access to that registry can extract your database credentials, API keys, and secret key — they can connect to your production database directly.

Explanation

Docker images are often pushed to public or shared registries (Docker Hub, ECR, GCR). Any file in the build context that isn't excluded by .dockerignore becomes part of the image layers — and anyone who can pull the image can extract those layers. Treat Docker images as potentially public artifacts.

Q6
Why use a non-root user in a container? What networking capabilities does root have that a regular user doesn't?
Show Answer
Answer

Root in a container can: (1) Bind to ports < 1024 (well-known ports like 80, 443). (2) Modify iptables rules (change routing, block/allowing traffic). (3) Change network interface configuration. (4) Open raw sockets (craft arbitrary packets). (5) Access /proc/net/* without restrictions. Why non-root: If an attacker compromises a root container, they can escape the container more easily, modify host networking, and pivot to other containers. A non-root user limits the attack surface. Bind to port 80 as non-root: Use setcap 'cap_net_bind_service=+ep' or use a higher port (8000, 8080) with a reverse proxy handling port 80/443.

Explanation

In Kubernetes, you can enforce non-root with securityContext: { runAsNonRoot: true }. Combined with readOnlyRootFilesystem: true and allowPrivilegeEscalation: false, you get a hardened container that's much harder to exploit.

Q7
How does layer caching in Dockerfiles work? How should you order COPY commands for optimal network-related rebuilds?
Show Answer
Answer

Docker caches each instruction as a layer. If a layer's inputs haven't changed, the cached layer is reused. Optimal ordering for fast rebuilds: (1) COPY requirements.txt (changes rarely → cached). (2) RUN pip install (cached unless requirements change). (3) COPY . (changes every build → this and everything after rebuilds). This means code changes only rebuild from the COPY . step — pip install is cached, saving minutes. Bad ordering: COPY . first → RUN pip install → code changes invalidate the entire build cache.

Explanation

This optimization is critical for CI/CD pipelines. A rebuild after changing one line of Django code should take seconds, not minutes. The networking dependency (COPY . being last) means your app's network configuration files are always fresh while dependencies are cached.

Q8
What happens when you run docker-compose up with the anihpj stack? Walk through the network setup.
Show Answer
Answer

(1) Compose creates two networks: anihpj_frontend and anihpj_backend. (2) Starts db and redis → waits for healthy status. (3) Starts web → attaches to BOTH frontend and backend networks. (4) Starts nginx → attaches to frontend only. (5) DNS becomes active: "db" resolves on backend, "web" resolves on both networks, "redis" resolves on backend. (6) nginx receives traffic on ports 80/443 → reverse proxies to web:8000 → web queries db:5432 over backend network. nginx CANNOT reach db directly — it's only on the frontend network.

Explanation

The network boundary between frontend and backend is the web container. It's the only service with interfaces on both networks. This creates a natural security boundary — even if nginx is compromised, the attacker has no direct network path to the database.

Q9
Your Dockerfile has COPY . . but the container shows old code. Why? How do you fix it?
Show Answer
Answer

Most likely: Docker is using a cached layer from a previous build where the code was in a different state. The cache is invalidated if any file in the build context has changed — but Docker doesn't always detect changes (especially with .dockerignore issues or timestamp-only changes). Fix: docker build --no-cache -t anihpj . (force full rebuild). Or add ARG CACHEBUST=unknown before COPY and build with --build-arg CACHEBUST=$(date +%s) to force cache invalidation at that point.

Explanation

Also check: (1) Is your code mounted as a volume? docker-compose.yml with volumes: - .:/app overrides the image's code with your local directory — changes appear live without rebuild. (2) Are you running the right image tag? docker ps shows which image is actually running.

Q10
How would you deploy the anihpj Dockerfile to Kubernetes? What networking changes are needed from Docker Compose?
Show Answer
Answer
# Key changes from Compose to K8s:
# 1. Service name → K8s Service DNS: db.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
# 2. depends_on → startupProbe/livenessProbe
# 3. Compose networks → K8s NetworkPolicies
# 4. Port mapping (-p 8000:8000) → K8s Service/Ingress
# 5. Volumes → PersistentVolumeClaims
# 6. Environment variables → ConfigMaps/Secrets

# Example K8s Service for the web deployment:
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: web
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  selector:
    app: web
  ports:
    - port: 8000
      targetPort: 8000

# NetworkPolicy for backend isolation:
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: db-isolation
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  podSelector:
    matchLabels:
      app: db
  ingress:
    - from:
        - podSelector:
            matchLabels:
              app: web
      ports:
        - port: 5432

The Compose project name becomes the K8s namespace. Compose networks become NetworkPolicies. Docker DNS becomes CoreDNS. The transition is natural because both use the same networking primitives (DNS, labels, selectors).

Explanation

Tools like Kompose can auto-convert docker-compose.yml to Kubernetes manifests: kompose convert -f docker-compose.yml. But manual conversion gives you more control — especially for NetworkPolicies, which don't have a direct Compose equivalent.

☸️ Part 6: Kubernetes Networking — The Full StackCh 18-23

Chapter 18: The Kubernetes Networking Model

Kubernetes networking is fundamentally different from Docker networking. While Docker focuses on single-host container connectivity, Kubernetes requires a flat, NAT-less network where every pod can communicate with every other pod across all nodes without Network Address Translation. This is achieved through the CNI (Container Network Interface) — a pluggable system that lets you choose how pod networking works.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~2.5 hours☸️ Kubernetes🎯 Critical

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The 4 fundamental K8s networking requirements — and why they exist
  • ✅ CNI — how the plugin system works, from kubelet to pod network
  • ✅ Deep comparison of CNI plugins: Calico, Cilium, Flannel, Weave
  • ✅ Overlay vs underlay networking — VXLAN encapsulation deep dive

18.1 The 4 Fundamental Networking Requirements

Kubernetes mandates four networking rules that every CNI plugin must satisfy:

1️⃣
Pods Can Communicate Across Nodes Without NAT

Pod A on Node 1 (10.244.1.5) can reach Pod B on Node 2 (10.244.2.8) directly — no NAT, no port mapping. The source IP the destination sees is the pod's real IP. This is the hardest requirement and what CNI plugins solve.

2️⃣
Node Agents Can Communicate With All Pods

kubelet, kube-proxy, and daemonsets on a node can reach all pods on that node directly. This enables health checks (kubelet → pod), log collection, and monitoring without NAT.

3️⃣
Pods See Their Own IP as Others See It

A pod sees its own IP (e.g., 10.244.1.5) as its source address. Other pods see the same IP. No NAT within the cluster — IP transparency for logging, tracing, and network policies.

4️⃣
Host-Network Pods Communicate Without NAT

Pods using hostNetwork: true can communicate with all other pods on all nodes without NAT. These pods use the node's IP but can still reach the pod network.

💡 Why a Flat, NAT-Less Network? — The Design Philosophy

Kubernetes chose a flat network model for a fundamental reason: IP identity = pod identity. In traditional networks, NAT hides real IPs — making logging, tracing, and security policies nearly impossible. In Kubernetes, every pod has a unique, routable IP that IS its identity. NetworkPolicies work because pod IPs are stable within the pod's lifetime. Service meshes work because the real source IP is visible. This was a deliberate choice: complexity at the infrastructure layer (CNI) in exchange for simplicity at the application layer (apps just use IPs). The alternative — NAT'd container networks like Docker's default bridge — works for single-host but collapses at cluster scale because IPs are no longer unique or routable across nodes.

💡 Why These 4 Rules Exist — The Design Philosophy Behind Flat Networking

These rules weren't chosen arbitrarily. Rule 1 (no NAT between pods): If pods were NAT'd, every pod would see traffic from the node's IP — making it impossible to distinguish which pod sent a request. NetworkPolicies would be useless because every request would appear to come from the node. Service meshes would break because mTLS identity is tied to pod IP. Distributed tracing would show all spans originating from node IPs. Rule 2 (node agents can reach pods): The kubelet must be able to call each pod's health check endpoint directly — without this, liveness/readiness probes fail. kube-proxy must reach pod IPs to populate Endpoints. Log collectors must reach pod filesystems. Rule 3 (pods see their real IP): If a pod saw a different source IP than what other pods see, applications would log incorrect IPs, rate limiters would target wrong clients, and any IP-based authentication would break. Rule 4 (host-network pods can reach pod network): Host-network pods (kube-proxy, CNI agents, monitoring daemonsets) need to reach the pod network to do their jobs. Without this, the entire cluster control plane couldn't function.

18.2 CNI — The Plugin That Makes It Work

CNI (Container Network Interface) is a specification, not a product. It defines how the container runtime (containerd/CRI-O) calls a plugin to set up networking for a pod. The flow: kubelet → CRI → CNI binary → IPAM → configure pod netns.

Kubernetes CNI Execution Flow — From Pod Creation to Network Ready
  1. kubelet receives PodSpec (from API server)
  2. kubelet calls CRI (Container Runtime Interface): "create pod"
  3. CRI (containerd) creates pod sandbox (pause container + netns)
  4. CRI calls CNI plugin with CNI_ARGS (pod name, namespace,
     container ID, network namespace path)
  5. CNI plugin:
     a. Calls IPAM plugin → get IP for this pod (e.g., 10.244.1.5)
     b. Creates veth pair: one end in pod netns, one on host
     c. Assigns IP to pod's eth0 (inside pod netns)
     d. Adds routes (default via bridge/host)
     e. Adds iptables rules (network policy, masquerade)
  6. CNI plugin reports result (IP, routes, DNS) back to CRI
  7. CRI returns success → kubelet updates pod status → Pod Running
CNI PluginEncapsulationNetworkPolicyEncryptioneBPFBest For
CalicoBGP/IPIP/VXLAN✅WireGuard✅Production K8s with NetworkPolicy
CiliumVXLAN/Geneve✅WireGuard/IPSec✅eBPF-native, Hubble observability
FlannelVXLAN/HostGW❌❌❌Simple, minimal setup
AWS VPC CNINative (ENI IPs)❌*❌❌EKS — pod IPs from VPC subnet

Calico (eBPF + BGP)

Calico is the most popular CNI for production Kubernetes. It supports multiple dataplanes depending on your network environment.

Calico — Three Dataplane Modes
  BGP MODE (underlay — fastest):
  ┌──────────┐  BGP: "I can reach 10.244.1.0/24"  ┌──────────┐
  │ Node 1   │◀══════════════════════════════════▶│ Node 2   │
  │10.244.1.x│  Pod IPs routable on physical net   │10.244.2.x│
  └──────────┘  No encapsulation! Zero overhead.    └──────────┘
  Requires BGP on routers/switches (or Calico Route Reflectors).

  IPIP/VXLAN MODE (overlay — cloud-friendly):
  ┌──────────┐  IPIP: pod packet inside IP packet  ┌──────────┐
  │ Node 1   │═════════════════════════════════════│ Node 2   │
  │10.244.1.x│  or VXLAN: pod packet inside UDP    │10.244.2.x│
  └──────────┘  Works on ANY network (no BGP req)  └──────────┘

  eBPF MODE (kernel-native):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Pod A ──▶ eBPF map lookup ──▶ Pod B                    │
  │  No iptables. No kube-proxy. Direct kernel routing.     │
  │  WireGuard encryption at kernel level.                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Cilium (eBPF-Native)

Cilium uses eBPF to program the Linux kernel directly — no iptables, no proxies per service.

Cilium — eBPF Data Path & Hubble Observability
  CILIUM eBPF DATA PATH (kernel-level, no iptables):
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ POD A (10.244.1.5)          POD B (10.244.2.8)            │
  │  │                           ▲                            │
  │  │ packet sent               │ packet received            │
  │  ▼                           │                            │
  │ ┌──────────────────────┐     │                            │
  │ │ eBPF Program (TC hook)│─────┼── Direct kernel routing   │
  │ │ • Service lookup      │     │  No iptables chain walk   │
  │ │ • NetworkPolicy check │     │  Per-endpoint LB          │
  │ │ • Encapsulation       │     │                            │
  │ │ • Encryption (WG)     │     │                            │
  │ └──────────────────────┘     │                            │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  HUBBLE OBSERVABILITY (built-in, zero-config):
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ┌─────────┐   ┌─────────┐   ┌─────────┐                  │
  │  │ jobpost │──▶│postgres │──▶│  redis  │                  │
  │  │ 45ms    │   │ 30ms    │   │  2ms    │                  │
  │  └─────────┘   └─────────┘   └─────────┘                  │
  │  Service map + latency + packet drops + HTTP status       │
  │  L7 POLICIES: allow GET /api/*, deny DELETE /admin/*       │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Flannel (VXLAN — Simplest Option)

Flannel is the simplest CNI — designed to "just work" with minimal configuration.

Flannel VXLAN — Node Subnet Allocation
  FLANNEL OVERLAY NETWORK (10.244.0.0/16):

  ┌─────────────────────────┐    ┌─────────────────────────┐
  │ NODE 1 (192.168.1.10)   │    │ NODE 2 (192.168.1.11)   │
  │ Pod CIDR: 10.244.1.0/24 │    │ Pod CIDR: 10.244.2.0/24 │
  │                         │    │                         │
  │ Pod A: 10.244.1.2       │    │ Pod C: 10.244.2.2       │
  │ Pod B: 10.244.1.3       │    │ Pod D: 10.244.2.3       │
  │ ... up to 254 pods      │    │ ... up to 254 pods      │
  │                         │    │                         │
  │ flannel.1 (VXLAN dev)   │    │ flannel.1 (VXLAN dev)   │
  └───────────┬─────────────┘    └───────────┬─────────────┘
              │                               │
              └──── VXLAN tunnel ─────────────┘
                   (UDP port 8472)

  SIMPLE: One DaemonSet, one ConfigMap, pods get IPs.
  LIMITS: No NetworkPolicy, no encryption, no eBPF.
  UPGRADE PATH: Keep Flannel for networking + add Calico for policy.

Weave Net

Weave creates a full mesh overlay — every node connects directly to every other node via UDP tunnels.

Weave Net — Full Mesh Overlay with Fast Datapath
  WEAVE FULL MESH (every node ↔ every node):

       ┌──────────┐
       │  Node 1  │
       │10.32.0.x │
       └────┬─┬───┘
            │ │
     ┌──────┘ └──────┐
     ▼               ▼
  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐
  │  Node 2  │◀─│  Node 3  │  All nodes peer directly!
  │10.44.0.x │  │10.48.0.x │  No central switch/bridge.
  └──────────┘  └──────────┘

  WEAVE ROUTER (on each node):
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ • Discovers peers via multicast/broadcast  │
  │ • Establishes UDP tunnels to all peers     │
  │ • Encrypts with NaCl (fast, secure)        │
  │ • Fast datapath: bypasses Linux bridge     │
  │   (kernel module for wire-speed)           │
  │ • NetworkPolicy via ODP (Open vSwitch)     │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  POD CIDR: 10.32.0.0/12 (default, configurable)
  Smaller community than Calico/Cilium but battle-tested.
💡 CNI IPAM — How Pods Get Their IPs

IPAM (IP Address Management) is the CNI sub-plugin that allocates IPs. host-local: Each node gets a /24 subnet, pods get sequential IPs (Flannel default). calico-ipam: IP pools with block affinity — each node gets a /26 block (62 pod IPs), allocation is persisted. AWS VPC IPAM: Pods get actual VPC IPs from ENI secondary addresses — no overlay needed. ⚠️ IP exhaustion: Pod stays "ContainerCreating" with "failed to allocate IP." AWS t3.medium = 2 ENIs × 3 IPs = 4 pod IPs (after subtracting 2 primary). Use larger instances or switch CNI for more pods per node.

⚠️ NetworkPolicy — Kubernetes Firewall Fundamentals

A NetworkPolicy is an L3/L4 firewall for pods — think "security group per pod." Pods are selected by labels; traffic is controlled by ingress/egress rules specifying: from/to pods (podSelector), namespaces (namespaceSelector), or IP blocks (ipBlock). ⚠️ CRITICAL: If NO NetworkPolicy selects a pod, ALL traffic is allowed (default-allow). Once ANY policy selects a pod, it becomes default-DENY — only traffic explicitly allowed by policies is permitted. ⚠️ NetworkPolicies are namespace-scoped. A policy in namespace "anihpj" only affects pods there. ⚠️ The CNI MUST support NetworkPolicy: Calico ✅, Cilium ✅, Flannel ❌ (add Calico for policy), AWS VPC CNI ❌.

18.3 Overlay vs Underlay — How Pod Networks Actually Work

Overlay: Pod traffic is encapsulated inside another packet (VXLAN, Geneve). The original pod packet becomes payload inside a UDP packet sent between nodes. Underlay: Pod IPs are routable on the physical network — no encapsulation. BGP or static routes tell the physical network how to reach pod subnets.

VXLAN Encapsulation — What Actually Hits the Wire
  OUTER ETHERNET │ OUTER IP │ UDP  │ VXLAN │ INNER ETHERNET + IP + TCP + HTTP
  (node MACs)    │(node to  │(4789)│(VNI)  │ (original pod packet)
                 │  node)   │      │       │
  ───────────────┼──────────┼──────┼───────┼────────────────────────────────
  14 bytes       │ 20 bytes │8 bytes│8 bytes│ original pod packet (up to 1450)

  TOTAL OVERHEAD = 14 + 20 + 8 + 8 = 50 bytes
  Max pod MTU on 1500 MTU link = 1500 - 50 = 1450 bytes

  ⚠️ If firewall blocks UDP port 4789 between nodes → cross-node pod traffic breaks SILENTLY!

18.4 [LAB 🛠️] CNI Exploration & Troubleshooting Lab

# === PHASE 1: Identify your CNI ===
kubectl get pods -n kube-system | grep -E 'calico|cilium|flannel|weave'
kubectl get nodes -o wide                       # PodCIDR per node
kubectl describe pod <cni-pod> -n kube-system    # CNI pod details

# === PHASE 2: Inspect CNI configuration (on a node) ===
ls /etc/cni/net.d/                               # CNI config files (.conf/.conflist)
cat /etc/cni/net.d/*.conf                        # Read the config
ls /opt/cni/bin/                                 # CNI plugin binaries
# Each binary = one CNI command (ADD/DEL/CHECK)

# === PHASE 3: Troubleshooting — pod stuck in ContainerCreating ===
kubectl describe pod <stuck-pod>                 # Events show CNI errors
kubectl logs -n kube-system <cni-pod> --tail=50  # CNI pod logs
# Common errors in logs:
# "failed to allocate IP" → IP pool exhausted (node CIDR full)
# "failed to find plugin" → /opt/cni/bin/ missing binary
# "timeout" → CNI socket unreachable

# === PHASE 4: Check CNI health ===
kubectl get pods -n kube-system -l k8s-app=calico-node
kubectl exec -n kube-system <cni-pod> -- <health-check-cmd>
# Calico: calicoctl node status
# Cilium: cilium status; cilium connectivity test

📝 Chapter 18 — Networking Practice Questions

Q1
What are the 4 fundamental Kubernetes networking requirements?
Show Answer
Answer

(1) Pods can communicate with all other pods on all nodes without NAT. (2) Node agents (kubelet) can communicate with all pods on that node. (3) Pods see their own IP as the IP others see them as. (4) Host-network pods can communicate with all pods without NAT. These requirements create a flat, NAT-less network where every pod has a unique, routable IP.

Q2
What is CNI? Walk through the CNI execution flow when a pod is created.
Show Answer
Answer

CNI is a specification defining how container runtimes call plugins to configure pod networking. Flow: kubelet → CRI → CNI binary (with pod name, namespace, netns path) → IPAM (allocates IP) → creates veth pair → assigns IP to eth0 → adds routes → adds iptables rules → reports result back. The CNI config file at /etc/cni/net.d/*.conf specifies which plugin and configuration to use.

Q3
Compare Calico and Cilium as CNI plugins. When would you choose one over the other?
Show Answer
Answer

Calico: Mature, BGP-native, simpler architecture, excellent NetworkPolicy, WireGuard encryption. Best for: teams that want battle-tested stability, BGP networking, or simpler operations. Cilium: eBPF-native, Hubble observability, L7 network policies (HTTP/Kafka/gRPC), Cluster Mesh. Best for: teams that want cutting-edge performance, deep observability, or application-layer security policies. Both support eBPF kube-proxy replacement.

Q4
What is the difference between overlay and underlay networking in Kubernetes?
Show Answer
Answer

Overlay: Pod packets are encapsulated (VXLAN/Geneve) inside UDP packets between nodes. 50-byte overhead. Works on any network — the physical network only sees node-to-node UDP. Underlay: Pod IPs are routable on the physical network — BGP or static routes tell routers how to reach pod subnets. No encapsulation overhead. Faster but requires network configuration (BGP on switches or cloud route tables). Calico BGP mode is the most common underlay approach.

Q5
What happens if UDP port 4789 is blocked between K8s nodes running Flannel VXLAN?
Show Answer
Answer

Cross-node pod-to-pod communication breaks SILENTLY. Pods on the same node can still communicate (local bridge). Pods on different nodes see timeouts. There's no error message — packets are simply dropped by the firewall. Debug: tcpdump -i eth0 'udp port 4789' on both nodes — you'll see packets sent but not received. Fix: Open UDP 4789 in firewall/Security Group between all K8s nodes. VXLAN must be allowed for overlay networking to function.

Q6
Why does Flannel not support NetworkPolicy? How can you add policy support?
Show Answer
Answer

Flannel focuses solely on pod networking (IP assignment + cross-node routing). It doesn't implement any network policy engine. Fix: Install Calico for policy only — this is a common pattern: Flannel for networking + Calico for NetworkPolicy. Calico's Felix agent enforces policies via iptables while Flannel handles IP allocation and routing. Alternatively, switch to Calico or Cilium which provide both networking and policy natively.

Q7
What is the pause container? Why does every pod have one?
Show Answer
Answer

The pause container is a minimal container (literally just runs pause() system call) that creates and holds the pod's network namespace. All other containers in the pod share this namespace. If the pause container dies, the pod's network namespace is destroyed. It's the "anchor" that keeps the pod's network alive regardless of what happens to application containers. The pause container also holds the PID namespace and IPC namespace.

Q8
What is IPAM in CNI? What happens when a node runs out of pod IPs?
Show Answer
Answer

IPAM allocates IP addresses to pods. Types: host-local: Each node gets a /24 subnet (254 pods max). Sequential allocation. calico-ipam: IP pools with block affinity — each node gets a /26 (62 pods). AWS VPC IPAM: Pods get ENI secondary IPs from VPC subnet. Exhaustion: Pod stays "ContainerCreating" with "failed to allocate IP" in Events. Fix: reduce pods per node, use larger pod CIDR, or switch IPAM strategy.

Q9
How does eBPF (Cilium) replace kube-proxy? What's the benefit?
Show Answer
Answer

Cilium attaches eBPF programs directly to the kernel's networking hooks (TC — Traffic Control). When a pod sends a packet to a Service ClusterIP, the eBPF program intercepts it BEFORE it hits iptables and directly rewrites the destination to a backend pod IP — O(1) constant time, no rule chains to traverse. Benefits: No iptables rule explosion, no connection resets during updates, per-packet load balancing, direct pod-to-pod routing, and built-in observability (Hubble). This eliminates the single biggest scaling bottleneck in large Kubernetes clusters.

Q10
Your new pod is stuck in ContainerCreating with "failed to set up pod network." Walk through debugging.
Show Answer
Answer
kubectl describe pod <pod>            # Events show the error
kubectl get pods -n kube-system | grep -E 'calico|cilium|flannel'
kubectl logs -n kube-system <cni-pod> --tail=50
# SSH to node: ls /etc/cni/net.d/     # Config present?
# ls /opt/cni/bin/                    # Binary present?
# Check: IP pool exhausted? Node taint blocking CNI?
# For AWS EKS: VPC subnet too small?

Common causes: CNI pod not running, CNI socket unreachable, IP pool exhausted (node CIDR full), node taint preventing CNI scheduling, or VPC subnet too small (AWS EKS ENI limits).

Chapter 19: Pod Networking — Life of a Packet in Kubernetes

Understanding exactly how a packet travels from one pod to another — on the same node or across nodes — is essential for debugging connectivity issues. This chapter traces the full packet path through network namespaces, veth pairs, bridges, and VXLAN tunnels.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1.5 hours🔧 Debugging

19.1 Inside a Pod's Network Namespace

Every pod gets its own network namespace. The pause container creates and holds it. All other containers in the pod share this namespace — they see the same interfaces (eth0, lo), same routing table, same iptables rules. A veth pair connects the pod's namespace to the host's root namespace.

Pod Networking — The Full Path
  POD A (on Node 1)                             POD B (on Node 2)
  ┌──────────────────┐                        ┌──────────────────┐
  │  Container 1     │                        │  Container 1     │
  │  gunicorn:8000   │                        │  nginx:80        │
  │  Container 2     │                        └────────┬─────────┘
  │  envoy sidecar   │                                 │
  └────────┬─────────┘                                 │
           │ (shared lo)                               │
  ┌────────┴─────────┐                        ┌────────┴─────────┐
  │  POD NETNS       │                        │  POD NETNS       │
  │  eth0:10.244.1.5 │                        │  eth0:10.244.2.8 │
  └────────┬─────────┘                        └────────┬─────────┘
           │ veth                                       │ veth
  ┌────────┴─────────┐                        ┌────────┴─────────┐
  │  NODE ROOT NETNS │                        │  NODE ROOT NETNS │
  │  cni0 bridge     │                        │  cni0 bridge     │
  │  (10.244.1.1)    │                        │  (10.244.2.1)    │
  └────────┬─────────┘                        └────────┬─────────┘
           │ eth0                                      │ eth0
           │ 192.168.1.10                              │ 192.168.1.11
           └──────────── VXLAN TUNNEL ─────────────────┘

19.2 Pod-to-Pod Communication on the Same Node

Same-node communication is simple: Pod A → veth → cni0 bridge (L2) → veth → Pod B. The cni0 bridge acts as a virtual switch — it learns MAC addresses from ARP and forwards frames directly.

Same-Node Packet Walk-Through — Pod A → Pod B on Node 1
  POD A (10.244.1.5)                          POD B (10.244.1.6)
  ┌────────────────┐                          ┌────────────────┐
  │ 1. App sends   │                          │ 6. Packet      │
  │    packet to   │                          │    delivered   │
  │    10.244.1.6  │                          │    to app      │
  │    ↓           │                          │    ↑           │
  │    eth0        │                          │    eth0        │
  └────┬───────────┘                          └────┬───────────┘
       │ veth (pod side)                           │ veth (pod side)
  ─────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────
       │ veth (host side)                          │ veth (host side)
  ┌────┴───────────┐                          ┌────┴───────────┐
  │ 2. Frame enters│                          │ 5. cni0 forwards│
  │    cni0 bridge │                          │    to dest veth │
  └────┬───────────┘                          └────┬───────────┘
       │         ┌──────────────────┐              │
       └────────▶│ 3. cni0 BRIDGE  │──────────────┘
                 │    ARP: who has  │  ARP reply: MAC of Pod B
                 │    10.244.1.6?   │→ learned, frame forwarded
                 │ 4. MAC table:    │
                 │    Pod A→vethA   │
                 │    Pod B→vethB   │
                 └──────────────────┘
  No encapsulation needed — pure L2 forwarding. Fastest pod-to-pod path.

19.3 Pod-to-Pod Communication Across Nodes

Cross-node communication requires routing and encapsulation. The CNI plugin adds routes for remote pod subnets pointing to destination node IPs.

Cross-Node Packet Walk-Through — Pod A (Node 1) → Pod B (Node 2)
  NODE 1 (192.168.1.10)                        NODE 2 (192.168.1.11)
  ┌──────────────────┐                         ┌──────────────────┐
  │ POD A:10.244.1.5 │                         │ POD B:10.244.2.8 │
  │ 1. Sends to      │                         │ 7. Packet        │
  │    10.244.2.8    │                         │    delivered!    │
  └────────┬─────────┘                         └────────┬─────────┘
           │ eth0 → veth                                │ eth0 ← veth
  ┌────────┴─────────┐                         ┌────────┴─────────┐
  │ 2. cni0 bridge   │                         │ 6. cni0 bridge   │
  └────────┬─────────┘                         └────────┬─────────┘
           │ route lookup                               │
  ┌────────┴─────────┐                         ┌────────┴─────────┐
  │ 3. ip route:     │                         │ 5. VXLAN decap:  │
  │ 10.244.2.0/24    │                         │    strip outer   │
  │ via 192.168.1.11 │                         │    IP+UDP+VXLAN  │
  │ dev eth0         │                         │    → original pkt│
  └────────┬─────────┘                         └────────┬─────────┘
           │                                              │
  ┌────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────┐
  │ 4. VXLAN ENCAPSULATION (on wire):                               │
  │ ┌──────────────┬──────────────┬────────┬────────┬──────────────┐│
  │ │ Outer Eth    │ Outer IP     │ UDP    │ VXLAN  │ Original Pod ││
  │ │ Src:Node1MAC │ Src:192.168  │ :4789  │ VNI=1  │ Packet       ││
  │ │ Dst:Node2MAC │ .1.10        │        │        │ Src:10.244.  ││
  │ │              │ Dst:192.168  │        │        │ 1.5          ││
  │ │              │ .1.11        │        │        │ Dst:10.244.  ││
  │ │ 14B          │ 20B          │ 8B     │ 8B     │ 2.8 + data   ││
  │ └──────────────┴──────────────┴────────┴────────┴──────────────┘│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

19.4 [LAB 🛠️] Pod Networking Lab

kubectl get pod -o wide                    # Pod IPs and nodes
kubectl exec pod-A -- ping <pod-B-ip>       # Cross-node ping test
kubectl exec pod-A -- traceroute <pod-B-ip> # See the path
kubectl exec pod-A -- ip addr; ip route     # Pod's network config
kubectl exec pod-A -- cat /etc/resolv.conf  # DNS config
tcpdump -i cni0                             # Capture on node bridge

19.5 Debugging Pod Networking — The Engineer's Toolkit

# FROM WITHIN A POD:
kubectl exec <pod> -- ip addr show              # Pod's IP, interface state
kubectl exec <pod> -- ip route show             # Routing table (default route?)
kubectl exec <pod> -- ping <other-pod-ip>       # L3 reachability
kubectl exec <pod> -- nc -zv <svc> <port>       # L4 TCP test
kubectl exec <pod> -- dig <svc>.<ns>.svc.cluster.local  # DNS test

# FROM THE NODE:
nsenter -t <pod-pid> -n ip addr              # Inspect pod netns from host
conntrack -L -s <pod-ip>                      # All connections for this pod
bridge fdb show br cni0                        # MAC table on the bridge

# ULTIMATE DEBUG POD:
kubectl run netshoot --rm -it --image=nicolaka/netshoot -- bash
# Contains: curl, dig, nc, tcpdump, iperf3, nmap, mtr, socat

19.6 K8s Pod Network Debugging Decision Tree

POD A CAN'T REACH POD B — Systematic Debug:
1. Are both pods Running? → kubectl get pods -o wide
2. Can pod A ping pod B's IP? → YES: L3 works, check L4/L7. NO: check CNI
3. Same node or different node? → kubectl get pods -o wide (NODE column)
   Same: check cni0 bridge → bridge fdb show br cni0
   Different: check VXLAN → tcpdump -i eth0 'udp port 4789'
4. NetworkPolicies blocking? → kubectl get networkpolicy -A
5. CNI pod logs → kubectl logs -n kube-system <cni-pod> --tail=100
6. Node routing correct? → SSH to each node, ip route | grep 10.244
7. tcpdump end-to-end: capture on src cni0, dst cni0, and VXLAN interface

📝 Chapter 19 — Networking Practice Questions

Q1
What is the pause container? Why is it critical for pod networking?
Show Answer
Answer

The pause container creates and holds the pod's shared network namespace. All other containers join this namespace. If the pause container stops, the namespace is destroyed, breaking all container networking. It also holds the PID and IPC namespaces. It runs the minimal /pause binary that simply blocks forever — its only job is to exist.

Q2
Walk through a packet from Pod A (10.244.1.5) to Pod B (10.244.2.8) on a different node.
Show Answer
Answer

Pod A sends packet → veth → cni0 bridge on Node 1 → route lookup: "10.244.2.0/24 via 192.168.1.11" → VXLAN encapsulation (wraps original packet in UDP:4789, outer IP: 192.168.1.10→192.168.1.11) → physical network → Node 2 receives → VXLAN decapsulation → cni0 bridge on Node 2 → veth → Pod B. The pod never knows about VXLAN — it just sends to 10.244.2.8 like any other IP.

Q3
What tools would you use to debug pod-to-pod connectivity issues? List the debugging decision tree.
Show Answer
Answer

Toolkit: kubectl exec with ping, nc, dig, traceroute; kubectl run netshoot; nsenter on nodes; conntrack; tcpdump on cni0 and VXLAN interfaces; CNI pod logs. Decision tree: (1) Pods Running? (2) Ping IP? Works=L3 OK, check L4/L7. (3) Same node? Check bridge. Different node? Check VXLAN. (4) NetworkPolicies? (5) CNI logs. (6) Node routing. (7) tcpdump end-to-end.

Q4
What is the netshoot container and why is it essential for network debugging?
Show Answer
Answer

Netshoot (nicolaka/netshoot) is a Docker image packed with every network debugging tool: curl, dig, nc, tcpdump, iperf3, nmap, mtr, socat, netcat-openbsd, tcptraceroute, ethtool. Launch it: kubectl run netshoot --rm -it --image=nicolaka/netshoot -- bash. It's the Swiss Army knife for pod network debugging — instead of installing tools on your app image, just launch a netshoot pod in the same namespace.

Q5
How does pod-to-pod communication differ on the same node vs across nodes?
Show Answer
Answer

Same node: Pod A → veth → cni0 bridge (L2 forwarding, ARP for MAC) → veth → Pod B. No encapsulation, no routing — just local bridge switching. Fastest path. Cross-node: Pod A → veth → cni0 → route lookup → VXLAN encapsulation → physical network → VXLAN decapsulation → cni0 → veth → Pod B. 50-byte VXLAN overhead, constrained by MTU. Same-node is ~10× faster (no network hop).

Q6
How can you inspect a pod's network namespace from the host node?
Show Answer
Answer
# Find pod's PID
POD_ID=$(docker ps | grep <pod-name> | awk '{print $1}')
PID=$(docker inspect $POD_ID | jq '.[0].State.Pid')
# Enter pod's netns
nsenter -t $PID -n ip addr
nsenter -t $PID -n ip route
nsenter -t $PID -n ss -tlnp
# Or with crictl (if using containerd)
crictl pods | grep <pod-name>
crictl inspect <pod-id> | jq '.info.runtimeSpec.linux.namespaces'
Q7
What is the cni0 bridge? What happens if you delete it?
Show Answer
Answer

cni0 is the Linux bridge created by most CNI plugins (Flannel, Calico IPIP mode) on each node. All pod veth pairs on that node plug into cni0 — it's the local switch for pod-to-pod communication. If you delete cni0, all pods on that node lose connectivity. The CNI plugin will recreate it on restart, but running pods may need to be restarted to reconnect. Never manually delete cni0 on a production node.

Q8
How does conntrack help debug pod networking? What commands show pod connections?
Show Answer
Answer
conntrack -L -s 10.244.1.5     # All connections FROM this pod
conntrack -L -d 10.96.100.50   # All connections TO this Service
conntrack -L | grep 10.244     # All pod-related connections
conntrack -S                   # Conntrack table statistics
# Watch for: "table full, dropping packet" in dmesg → increase nf_conntrack_max

Conntrack shows every NAT'd connection. Essential for: finding which backend a Service sent traffic to, checking if connections are being tracked, debugging connection drops due to table overflow.

Q9
Pod A can ping Pod B on the same node but not on a different node. What's the likely issue?
Show Answer
Answer

Most likely: (1) VXLAN port blocked: Firewall/Security Group blocking UDP 4789 between nodes. Same-node traffic uses the bridge directly — no VXLAN needed. Cross-node traffic requires VXLAN. Test: tcpdump -i eth0 'udp port 4789'. (2) Missing routes: Node 1 doesn't have a route to Node 2's pod subnet. Check: ip route | grep 10.244. (3) CNI pod not running on Node 2: Node 2's CNI agent is down. (4) Overlay network MTU mismatch: Packet too large after VXLAN encapsulation (PMTU black hole).

Q10
How does the kernel route pod traffic between nodes? Show the routing table entries.
Show Answer
Answer
ip route
# default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0
# 10.244.1.0/24 dev cni0 proto kernel scope link src 10.244.1.1    (LOCAL pods)
# 10.244.2.0/24 via 192.168.1.11 dev eth0                           (REMOTE pods)
# 10.244.3.0/24 via 192.168.1.12 dev eth0                           (REMOTE pods)

# The CNI plugin (Flannel/Calico) adds routes for REMOTE pod subnets.
# Each route points to the DESTINATION NODE's IP as the next hop.
# The CNI also sets up the VXLAN tunnel interface for encapsulation.

Chapter 20: Services — Stable Networking for Ephemeral Pods

Pods are ephemeral — they die, restart, and get new IPs. Services provide a stable virtual IP (ClusterIP) and DNS name that survives pod churn. They're the fundamental abstraction that makes distributed applications possible on Kubernetes, providing load balancing, service discovery, and stable endpoints.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~2 hours☸️ Kubernetes🎯 Critical

20.1 Why Services Exist — The Pod Ephemerality Problem

If you hardcode pod IPs in your config and the pod restarts, your app breaks. Services solve this: a selector matches pods by labels. Clients connect to the Service's stable ClusterIP (or DNS name). The Service load-balances to healthy backend pods. If a pod dies and is replaced, the Service automatically updates its endpoints.

Service TypeAccessible FromGets An IPTypical Use
ClusterIP (default)Inside cluster onlyInternal VIP (10.96.x.x)Pod-to-pod communication
NodePortOutside via NodeIP:PortClusterIP + NodeIP:PortDev access, simple exposure
LoadBalancerOutside via cloud LBClusterIP + cloud LB IPProduction internet-facing
ExternalNameVia DNS CNAMENo IP, just DNS nameExternal service proxy
Headless (clusterIP:None)Pod IPs directlyNo ClusterIPStatefulSet peer discovery
⚠️ A Service with no matching pods silently drops traffic

No error, no log — just timeouts. Always verify: kubectl get endpoints <svc-name>. If the ENDPOINTS column is empty, the selector matches no pods. Common cause: label mismatch between Service selector and pod labels.

💡 Endpoints vs EndpointSlices — Why the Old Way Doesn't Scale

Originally, every Service had one Endpoints object containing ALL pod IPs. Problem: at 5000 pods behind a Service, the Endpoints object was massive (5000 IPs in one resource). Every pod change → update the entire Endpoints → kube-proxy on EVERY node re-reads and rewrites all iptables rules. EndpointSlices (GA in 1.21) solves this: the 5000 pod IPs are split into multiple slices (100 IPs each). kube-proxy only watches the slices it needs. Pod changes only trigger updates to the affected slice — not the entire list. This dramatically reduces API server load, network traffic, and kube-proxy CPU at scale. From Kubernetes 1.21+, EndpointSlices are the default and strongly preferred.

20.2 ClusterIP — Internal-Only Services

The default Service type. Gets a virtual IP from the service CIDR (e.g., 10.96.0.0/12). This IP exists only in iptables/IPVS rules — it's not bound to any interface.

ClusterIP Service — iptables DNAT Chain Walk-Through
  CLIENT POD (10.244.1.5) → curl http://jobpost-svc:8000/api/jobs/
  │
  │ DNS: jobpost-svc → 10.96.100.50 (ClusterIP)
  ▼
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ PREROUTING                                                  │
  │  → KUBE-SERVICES                                           │
  │     Match: dst=10.96.100.50, dport=8000                     │
  │     Action: jump to KUBE-SVC-XXXX                           │
  │       → KUBE-SVC-XXXX (Service chain)                       │
  │          │ probability 0.333 → KUBE-SEP-AAA (Pod-1)         │
  │          │ probability 0.500 → KUBE-SEP-BBB (Pod-2)         │
  │          │ probability 0.999 → KUBE-SEP-CCC (Pod-3)         │
  │          │                                                  │
  │          → KUBE-SEP-AAA (Endpoint chain — Pod 1)             │
  │             DNAT: 10.96.100.50:8000 → 10.244.1.5:8000       │
  │                                                              │
  │  → POSTROUTING (MASQUERADE if needed)                       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  BACKEND PODS:                                          Service:
  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐       jobpost-svc
  │ Pod-1    │  │ Pod-2    │  │ Pod-3    │       10.96.100.50:8000
  │10.244.1.5│  │10.244.1.6│  │10.244.2.7│       Selector: app=jobpost
  │ :8000    │  │ :8000    │  │ :8000    │
  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘

20.3 kube-proxy Modes — iptables vs IPVS vs eBPF

FeatureiptablesIPVSeBPF (Cilium)
LookupO(n) — linear chainO(1) — hash tableO(1) — direct map
AlgorithmRandom (statistical)rr, lc, sh, dh, 10+ othersCustom, programmable
Scale~5000 services100K+ servicesUnlimited
Session AffinityYes (iptables recent)Yes (persistence)Yes
Update CostRewrite ALL rulesPer-service updatePer-endpoint update

iptables Mode (Default)

kube-proxy creates iptables rules: KUBE-SERVICES → KUBE-SVC-XXXX → KUBE-SEP-XXXX (one per endpoint). Random selection via statistical probability (not true round-robin). Why O(n): iptables evaluates rules sequentially — each packet traverses the chain rule by rule until it matches. With 50,000 rules, average traversal = 25,000 rule evaluations per packet. Problem at scale: 5000 services × 10 endpoints = 50,000 iptables rules. Every rule update rewrites ALL rules — causing connection resets during the rewrite window.

IPVS Mode (Scalable)

Uses Linux kernel's L4 load balancer (IP Virtual Server). Why O(1): IPVS uses a hash table — the kernel hashes the 5-tuple and looks up the backend in constant time, regardless of how many services exist. No rule chains to traverse. Supports 10+ scheduling algorithms: rr (round-robin), lc (least connection), sh (source hash), dh (destination hash). IPVS handles 100K+ services without degradation. Enable: kube-proxy --proxy-mode=ipvs. The difference at scale: iptables = 25,000 rule evaluations per packet; IPVS = 1 hash lookup. This is why IPVS is mandatory for large clusters.

Userspace Mode (Legacy — Never Use in Production)

This was kube-proxy's original mode (Kubernetes 1.0 era). Instead of programming the kernel (iptables/IPVS), kube-proxy runs as a user-space TCP proxy. How it works: kube-proxy opens a port on the node for each Service. When a client connects to that port, kube-proxy accepts the connection, opens a NEW connection to a backend pod, and manually copies data between the two connections (like netcat relay). Why it's terrible: Every packet travels: client → kernel → kube-proxy (user-space) → kernel → backend pod. That's two extra kernel↔user context switches per packet — compared to zero for iptables/IPVS modes. Latency: 10-50× higher. Throughput: limited by single-threaded event loop. Why it still exists: Fallback when iptables/IPVS kernel modules aren't available. Useful for debugging with --v=4 to see every connection proxied. Never use in production.

eBPF Mode (Cilium — No kube-proxy)

Cilium replaces kube-proxy entirely with eBPF programs attached to kernel hooks. Service lookup becomes a direct eBPF map lookup → O(1). Benefits: no iptables rules, no kube-proxy pod, built-in observability (Hubble), per-endpoint load balancing, connection tracking at eBPF level. This is the future of K8s networking.

20.4 NodePort — Exposing Services on Node IPs

Opens a port (30000-32767 by default) on EVERY node. Rarely used directly in production — mainly for dev access or as the underlying mechanism for LoadBalancer Services.

NodePort Service — How External Traffic Reaches Pods
  EXTERNAL CLIENT                      K8S CLUSTER
  ┌──────────────┐
  │ curl NodeIP  │──────┐
  │ :30080       │      │
  └──────────────┘      │
                         ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ NODE 1 (192.168.1.10)       NODE 2 (192.168.1.11)           │
  │                                                              │
  │ iptables KUBE-NODEPORTS:    iptables KUBE-NODEPORTS:        │
  │ DNAT :30080 →               DNAT :30080 →                   │
  │ KUBE-SVC-XXXX (ClusterIP)   KUBE-SVC-XXXX (ClusterIP)       │
  │   → KUBE-SEP-AAA (Pod-1)      → KUBE-SEP-BBB (Pod-3)        │
  │      DNAT → 10.244.1.5:8000     DNAT → 10.244.2.7:8000      │
  │                                                              │
  │ ┌──────────┐                 ┌──────────┐                   │
  │ │ Pod-1    │                 │ Pod-3    │                   │
  │ │:8000     │                 │ :8000    │                   │
  │ └──────────┘                 └──────────┘                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  # Port 30080 is open on EVERY node — even nodes with no local pods!
  # Traffic to Node2:30080 is DNAT'd to Pod-3 on Node2 (or cross-node).
  # NodePort range: 30000-32767 (configurable via --service-node-port-range)

20.5 LoadBalancer — Cloud-Native Service Exposure

Provisions a cloud load balancer (AWS NLB, GCP TCP LB). The LB sends traffic to NodePort on each node → ClusterIP → pod.

External Traffic to Pod — Complete Packet Journey
  🌍 Internet User (203.0.113.50:54321)
       │
       ▼
  ☁️ Cloud Load Balancer (AWS NLB: 34.120.45.67:80)
       │  LB chooses a healthy K8s node
       ▼
  K8s Node (192.168.1.10:30080 — NodePort)
       │  iptables DNAT: 192.168.1.10:30080 → 10.96.100.50:8000 (ClusterIP)
       ▼
  ClusterIP (10.96.100.50:8000 — virtual IP)
       │  iptables random: pick one backend pod
       ▼
  Pod (10.244.1.5:8000 — gunicorn serving jobpost API)

  ⚠️ externalTrafficPolicy=Cluster (default): SNAT hides client IP (pod sees node IP)
  ✅ externalTrafficPolicy=Local: Preserves client IP but only routes to LOCAL pods

  HOW SOURCE IP GETS LOST (Cluster mode):
  1. External client (203.0.113.50) → Cloud LB → Node 1 (192.168.1.10:30080)
  2. Node 1 DNATs to ClusterIP → randomly picks Pod B on Node 2 (cross-node!)
  3. Node 1 must forward to Node 2 → Source = Node 1 IP (SNAT), not client IP
  4. Pod B sees source = 192.168.1.10 (Node 1). Real client IP 203.0.113.50 is GONE.

  HOW LOCAL PRESERVES IT:
  externalTrafficPolicy: Local → traffic arriving at Node 1 ONLY goes to pods on Node 1.
  No cross-node hop needed → no SNAT → pod sees real client IP.
  Trade-off: If Node 1 has no local pods for this Service, traffic is DROPPED.

  REAL-WORLD IMPACT OF LOSING SOURCE IP:
  • Rate limiting: Pod sees Node IP → rate limits ALL traffic as one client
  • Geo-IP: Everyone appears to be from your node's subnet — useless for analytics
  • Audit logs: Can't identify which external user made which request
  • PCI/HIPAA compliance: Require source IP logging — Cluster mode fails this
  • Solution: externalTrafficPolicy: Local + pod on every node (DaemonSet)
  • Alternative: L7 Ingress proxy adds X-Forwarded-For header with real client IP

20.6 [LAB 🛠️] Services Lab

kubectl expose deployment jobpost --port=8000 --target-port=8000
kubectl get svc jobpost                     # Check ClusterIP
kubectl get endpoints jobpost               # Backend pod IPs
kubectl run test --rm -it --image=nicolaka/netshoot -- bash
curl http://jobpost.anihpj.svc.cluster.local:8000/api/jobs/
kubectl expose deployment jobpost --type=NodePort --port=8000
curl http://<node-ip>:<nodeport>/api/jobs/   # Access externally

20.7 Debugging kube-proxy — Tracing Service Traffic Through iptables

# See all ClusterIP DNAT rules
iptables -t nat -L KUBE-SERVICES -n
# See all NodePort rules
iptables -t nat -L KUBE-NODEPORTS -n
# See which backends a specific Service uses (with packet counters!)
iptables -t nat -L KUBE-SVC-XXXX -n -v
# See conntrack entries for this Service
conntrack -L -d 10.96.100.50

# ⚠️ iptables mode is RANDOM — not round-robin!
# 10 requests might go: backend-1: 7, backend-2: 2, backend-3: 1
# For even distribution, use IPVS mode with round-robin scheduler.

# ⚠️ iptables rules are NOT atomically updated.
# When a Service backend changes, kube-proxy rewrites ALL rules.
# At 5000+ services, this causes connection resets during rule updates.
# IPVS and eBPF modes don't have this problem.

# Full chain trace:
# PREROUTING → KUBE-SERVICES → KUBE-SVC-XXX → KUBE-SEP-XXX → POSTROUTING

📝 Chapter 20 — Networking Practice Questions

Q1
Why do Kubernetes Services exist? What problem do they solve?
Show Answer
Answer

Pods are ephemeral — they die, restart, and get new IPs. Services provide a STABLE virtual IP (ClusterIP) and DNS name that survives pod churn. They also provide L4 load balancing across backend pods and automatic endpoint management. Without Services, every time a pod restarted, all clients would need their connection configs updated.

Q2
What are the 5 Service types in Kubernetes? When would you use each?
Show Answer
Answer

ClusterIP: Internal-only. Pod-to-pod communication. NodePort: Exposes on every node's IP at a high port. Dev/staging access. LoadBalancer: Provisions cloud LB. Production internet-facing. ExternalName: CNAME to external DNS. Proxy to external services. Headless (clusterIP:None): No virtual IP — DNS returns pod IPs directly. StatefulSet peer discovery, databases.

Q3
How does kube-proxy in iptables mode implement a ClusterIP Service? Walk through the iptables chains.
Show Answer
Answer

Packet arrives at node → PREROUTING → KUBE-SERVICES chain (DNAT: ClusterIP:Port → randomly selected backend pod IP:targetPort) → KUBE-SVC-XXXX chain (statistical probability split across KUBE-SEP-XXXX endpoints) → KUBE-SEP-XXXX (DNAT to specific pod IP) → POSTROUTING. Each backend endpoint gets its own KUBE-SEP chain. Selection is random, not round-robin.

Q4
Compare iptables mode vs IPVS mode vs eBPF mode for kube-proxy.
Show Answer
Answer

iptables: O(n) chain traversal, random backend selection, ~5000 service limit before rule update latency causes connection resets. IPVS: O(1) hash lookup, 10+ scheduling algorithms (rr, lc, sh), scales to 100K+ services. eBPF (Cilium): O(1) map lookup, replaces kube-proxy entirely, per-endpoint load balancing, built-in observability. Switch to IPVS at 1000+ services. Switch to eBPF for maximum performance and observability.

Q5
What happens when you create a Service with no matching pods? How do you detect it?
Show Answer
Answer

Traffic is silently dropped — connections timeout. No error, no log message. Detection: kubectl get endpoints <svc> — if ENDPOINTS is empty, no pods match the selector. Common causes: Label mismatch (selector: app=web, but pod has app=nginx), pods not yet ready (readinessProbe failing), wrong namespace (Service and pods in different namespaces).

Q6
How does LoadBalancer Service type work end-to-end? Trace a packet from the internet to a pod.
Show Answer
Answer

Internet user → Cloud LB (AWS NLB provisioned by cloud-controller-manager) → K8s Node NodePort (30000-32767) → iptables DNAT to ClusterIP → iptables DNAT to random backend pod. The cloud LB does health checks against NodePort. With externalTrafficPolicy=Cluster (default), the source IP is SNAT'd (pod sees node IP). With Local, client IP is preserved but traffic only routes to pods on the receiving node.

Q7
You have 3 backend pods behind a Service. 10 requests come in. iptables mode sends 8 to pod-1, 2 to pod-2, 0 to pod-3. Why?
Show Answer
Answer

iptables mode uses STATISTICAL probability, not round-robin. Each KUBE-SEP rule has a probability: 1st = 1/3 (33%), 2nd = 1/2 (50% of remaining), 3rd = 100% (remaining). This is random — over small sample sizes, the distribution is uneven. Over thousands of requests, it converges to roughly equal. For guaranteed round-robin: use IPVS mode with rr scheduler. For even distribution with iptables: it works at scale — the unevenness you see at 10 requests is expected statistical variance.

Q8
What is externalTrafficPolicy? What's the difference between Cluster and Local?
Show Answer
Answer

Cluster (default): Traffic can go to any node and be routed to any pod (even on a different node). Source IP is SNAT'd — the pod sees the node's IP, not the client IP. Local: Traffic is only routed to pods on the node that received it. Source IP is PRESERVED. If a node receives traffic but has no local pods, the traffic is dropped. Use Local when you need client IP for logging/security. Use Cluster for even distribution across all pods.

Q9
How do you debug kube-proxy and trace which backend a Service sent traffic to?
Show Answer
Answer
iptables -t nat -L KUBE-SERVICES -n              # Find Service chain
iptables -t nat -L KUBE-SVC-XXXX -n -v           # See per-backend counters
conntrack -L -d <ClusterIP>                       # Active connections
kubectl logs -n kube-system kube-proxy-xxx        # Proxy logs
# Counters show packets/bytes per backend — tells you distribution
Q10
What is a headless Service? How does DNS work differently for headless vs regular Services?
Show Answer
Answer

A headless Service has clusterIP: None. Regular Service DNS: A record → ClusterIP (single IP). Headless Service DNS: A record → ALL pod IPs directly (DNS round-robin). SRV records for named ports. Used for: StatefulSets (each pod needs a stable network identity — pod-0.svc.ns.svc.cluster.local), databases where clients need to discover all replicas, Kafka/ZooKeeper peer discovery. The client gets pod IPs directly — no kube-proxy involvement.

Chapter 21: Ingress & Ingress Controllers

Services provide L4 (TCP/UDP) load balancing. Ingress adds L7 (HTTP/HTTPS) routing — path-based, host-based, TLS termination, and more. An Ingress resource is just configuration; an Ingress Controller (nginx, Traefik, AWS ALB) watches for these resources and configures itself to route traffic.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1.5 hours🌐 L7

21.1 Why Ingress Exists — Beyond Services

Services route by IP:Port (L4). Ingress adds L7 HTTP routing.

Ingress — L7 Routing with One Entry Point
  INTERNET
     │
     ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              INGRESS CONTROLLER (nginx)               │
  │              TLS Termination at Edge                  │
  │              Single External IP/LB                    │
  └──┬────────────────────┬──────────────────┬───────────┘
     │                    │                  │
     │ Host: api.anihpj.com   │ Host: admin.anihpj.com
     │ Path: /api/*           │ Path: /admin/*     Path: /
     ▼                    ▼                  ▼
  ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────┐
  │ jobpost  │       │  admin   │       │ frontend │
  │ Service  │       │ Service  │       │ Service  │
  │ :8000    │       │ :8001    │       │ :3000    │
  └──────────┘       └──────────┘       └──────────┘

  ONE external IP. ONE TLS cert. Routes to MANY Services by host + path.
  Without Ingress: each Service needs its own LoadBalancer ($$$ + complexity).
⚠️ The Ingress resource alone does NOTHING — it requires an Ingress Controller.

21.2 Ingress Controllers Compared

FeatureNGINXTraefikHAProxyAWS ALB
Config MethodAnnotations + ConfigMapK8s CRDs + annotationsConfigMap + annotationsAnnotations (ALB-specific)
Reconfigurationnginx reload (brief downtime)Dynamic — no reloadsRuntime API — no reloadsAWS API — fully dynamic
TLSSecret referenceBuilt-in Let's EncryptSecret referenceAWS Certificate Manager
Dashboard❌ (3rd party)✅ Built-in✅ Statistics page✅ AWS Console
Best ForUniversal, max communityPolish, small teamsHAProxy shops, L7 powerAWS-native deployments
Canary/Rate LimitingAnnotations (canary)✅ Middleware✅ Via config✅ ALB weighted groups

NGINX Ingress Controller

The most deployed Ingress controller — battle-tested across millions of clusters. Controller pod watches K8s API for Ingress resources → Go template renders nginx.conf → sends HUP signal to reload nginx. Configured entirely via annotations (rewrite-target, ssl-redirect, proxy-body-size, cors-allow-origin) and a ConfigMap for global settings. Downside: Every Ingress change triggers an nginx reload — at 100+ Ingresses with frequent changes, reloads cause brief connection drops. Use IngressClass or dynamic-config nginx variants (OpenResty-based) for large-scale deployments.

Traefik

Traefik auto-discovers Services, Ingresses, and IngressRoutes via the K8s API — no reloads needed. Ships with a built-in web dashboard showing real-time traffic, routes, and health. Let's Encrypt integration is native — enable TLS with one annotation. Middleware system supports: rate limiting, circuit breakers, retry, basic auth, headers manipulation, redirects — chainable in any order. Best for teams that want a polished, batteries-included experience with minimal operational overhead.

HAProxy Ingress

Brings HAProxy's legendary L7 routing and performance into Kubernetes. Supports: advanced ACL-based routing, request/response header manipulation, detailed per-backend metrics (queue, connect, response times). Dynamic reconfiguration via HAProxy Runtime API — no reloads, no connection drops. Best for organizations already running HAProxy at the edge who want consistent tooling inside K8s.

AWS ALB Ingress Controller

Instead of running a proxy in the cluster, this controller provisions AWS Application Load Balancers OUTSIDE the cluster. Each Ingress resource → one ALB with listener rules. Integrates natively with: AWS Certificate Manager (TLS certs, auto-renewal), AWS WAF (web application firewall at the edge), AWS Cognito (OIDC/OAuth authentication at the LB level — your app never sees auth). ALB targets pod IPs directly (IP mode) — eliminates the NodePort hop. Best for AWS-native deployments that want to minimize in-cluster components.

21.3 NGINX Ingress Controller Deep Dive

Architecture: Ingress Controller pod watches K8s API → Go template renders nginx.conf → nginx reload.

NGINX Ingress Controller — Watch Loop and Config Generation
  ┌──────────┐     ┌─────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────┐
  │ K8s API  │────▶│ Ingress Ctrl    │────▶│ nginx.conf       │
  │          │     │ Pod (Go app)    │     │ template (Go tmpl)│
  │ Ingress  │     │                 │     │                  │
  │ Service  │     │ Watch Ingress,  │     │ Render:          │
  │ Endpoints│     │ Service, Secret │     │ server {         │
  │ Secrets  │     │ changes         │     │   listen 443;    │
  └──────────┘     └────────┬────────┘     │   server_name..; │
                            │               │   location /api {│
                            ▼               │     proxy_pass..;│
                     ┌──────────────┐       │   }              │
                     │ nginx reload │       │ }                │
                     │ (HUP signal) │       └────────┬─────────┘
                     └──────────────┘                │
                            │                        ▼
                            ▼               ┌──────────────────┐
                     ⚠️ Brief downtime       │ /etc/nginx/      │
                     during reload           │ nginx.conf       │
                     (milliseconds)          └──────────────────┘

  Key annotations:
  nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/rewrite-target: /
  nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/ssl-redirect: "true"
  nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/proxy-body-size: 8m
  nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/proxy-read-timeout: "60"

21.4 TLS with Ingress — cert-manager Integration

cert-manager automates TLS certificate lifecycle from request to renewal.

cert-manager Workflow — Ingress → Certificate → Let's Encrypt → Secret
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1. YOU CREATE: Ingress with annotation                           │
  │    cert-manager.io/cluster-issuer: "letsencrypt-prod"             │
  │    tls: [{hosts: [jobpost.anihpj.com], secretName: jobpost-tls}]│
  └──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┘
                             │ cert-manager watches
                             ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 2. CERT-MANAGER CREATES: CertificateRequest                      │
  │    → Order (ACME order with Let's Encrypt)                       │
  │    → Challenge (HTTP-01: serve token at /.well-known/acme-challenge/)│
  └──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┘
                             │ Let's Encrypt verifies token
                             ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 3. LET'S ENCRYPT ISSUES: Certificate                             │
  │    → cert-manager stores in K8s Secret: anihpj-tls               │
  │    → Secret contains: tls.crt (full chain) + tls.key (private)  │
  └──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┘
                             │
                             ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 4. INGRESS REFERENCES: tls: [{secretName: anihpj-tls}]           │
  │    → nginx loads cert + key → serves HTTPS                      │
  │    → Auto-renewal: cert-manager renews 30 days before expiry    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ⚠️ HTTP-01 requires port 80 publicly reachable.
  Blocked? Use DNS-01: cert-manager creates TXT record via Route53/Cloudflare API.

📝 Chapter 21 — Networking Practice Questions

Q1
What's the difference between a Service (L4) and an Ingress (L7)?
Show Answer
Answer

Service (L4): Routes by IP:Port. One Service = one backend. No path/host routing, no TLS termination. Ingress (L7): Routes by HTTP host/path. One Ingress can route to multiple Services. Built-in TLS termination. Path-based (/api/* → backend-A), host-based (api.example.com → one Service). Ingress is an HTTP reverse proxy configuration; Service is a TCP/UDP load balancer.

Q2
An Ingress resource exists but traffic doesn't route. What's the most likely cause?
Show Answer
Answer

No Ingress Controller is installed. The Ingress resource is just configuration — it does nothing without a controller. Install one: nginx, Traefik, or cloud-specific. Check: kubectl get pods -A | grep ingress. Also check: (1) DNS points to the controller's IP/LB. (2) IngressClass matches between Ingress and Controller. (3) The backend Service exists and has healthy endpoints.

Q3
How does cert-manager automate TLS certificates for Ingress?
Show Answer
Answer

Annotate Ingress with cert-manager.io/cluster-issuer: letsencrypt-prod → cert-manager creates CertificateRequest → performs ACME HTTP-01 challenge (places token at /.well-known/acme-challenge/) → Let's Encrypt verifies → issues certificate → stores in K8s Secret → Ingress references Secret. Auto-renews 30 days before expiry. DNS-01 challenge alternative for wildcard certs or when port 80 is blocked.

Q4
Why can multiple Ingress Controllers run in the same cluster? How do you avoid conflicts?
Show Answer
Answer

Use IngressClass to designate which controller handles each Ingress. Without IngressClass, ALL controllers try to handle ALL Ingress resources — causing conflicts (multiple controllers updating the same Ingress status). Set spec.ingressClassName: nginx on the Ingress to specify which controller. Controllers watch only Ingresses matching their IngressClass. Default IngressClass can be set as cluster default.

Q5
You have 100+ Ingress resources. NGINX Ingress Controller reloads on every change, causing downtime. How do you fix this?
Show Answer
Answer

The root cause: nginx reloads on every Ingress change. Solutions: (1) Switch to Traefik or HAProxy Ingress — they use dynamic reconfiguration APIs (no reloads). (2) Use NGINX Ingress Controller with "dynamic configuration" mode (Lua/OpenResty) if available. (3) Use Envoy-based controllers (Contour, Emissary) — xDS protocol for dynamic updates. (4) Split Ingresses across multiple controllers using IngressClass. (5) Reduce Ingress change frequency (batch updates).

Q6
What are common NGINX Ingress annotations and what do they do?
Show Answer
Answer

nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/rewrite-target: / — rewrite URL path. ssl-redirect: "true" — force HTTPS. proxy-body-size: 8m — max upload size. proxy-read-timeout: "60" — backend response timeout. cors-allow-origin: "*" — CORS header. rate-limiting annotations — per-IP rate limits. whitelist-source-range — IP allowlist. All configuration happens via annotations — no separate config files needed.

Q7
What is the TLS Secret format for an Ingress? How does cert-manager create it?
Show Answer
Answer
apiVersion: v1
kind: Secret
metadata:
  name: jobpost-tls
type: kubernetes.io/tls
data:
  tls.crt: <base64-encoded-certificate>
  tls.key: <base64-encoded-private-key>

cert-manager creates this automatically. The Ingress references it: tls: [{ hosts: [jobpost.anihpj.com], secretName: jobpost-tls }]. The cert includes the full chain (leaf + intermediates). cert-manager handles renewal transparently.

Q8
Compare NGINX Ingress Controller with Traefik for a production Kubernetes cluster.
Show Answer
Answer

NGINX: Most deployed, huge community, battle-tested. Config via annotations + ConfigMap. Reload on every change (problem at scale). Traefik: Dynamic config (no reloads), built-in dashboard, Let's Encrypt, middleware chaining. Smaller community. Choose NGINX: You want the safest choice with maximum community support. Choose Traefik: You want polish, dynamic reconfiguration, and built-in observability out of the box.

Q9
How does AWS ALB Ingress Controller differ from NGINX Ingress Controller architecturally?
Show Answer
Answer

NGINX Ingress: Runs as pods in the cluster. Traffic flows: Client → cloud LB (NLB) → NGINX pod → Service → backend pod. TLS terminated at NGINX pod. AWS ALB Controller: Provisions an AWS Application Load Balancer OUTSIDE the cluster. Traffic flows: Client → ALB → directly to pod IPs (via ENI). TLS terminated at ALB (AWS Certificate Manager). ALB doesn't run in the cluster — it's a managed AWS service. ALB targets pod IPs directly (IP mode), not NodePort. This eliminates a network hop compared to NGINX.

Q10
Design an Ingress configuration for anihpj that routes /api/* to the jobpost Service and /admin/* to the admin Service, with TLS.
Show Answer
Answer
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Ingress
metadata:
  name: anihpj
  annotations:
    cert-manager.io/cluster-issuer: letsencrypt-prod
    nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/rewrite-target: /
spec:
  ingressClassName: nginx
  tls:
    - hosts: [jobpost.anihpj.com]
      secretName: anihpj-tls
  rules:
    - host: jobpost.anihpj.com
      http:
        paths:
          - path: /api
            pathType: Prefix
            backend:
              service:
                name: jobpost-service
                port:
                  number: 8000
          - path: /admin
            pathType: Prefix
            backend:
              service:
                name: admin-service
                port:
                  number: 8001

Chapter 22: Gateway API — The Next-Gen Ingress

Gateway API is the successor to Ingress — a more expressive, role-oriented, and extensible API for routing traffic in Kubernetes. It separates concerns: infrastructure teams manage Gateways, application teams manage Routes. Gateway API supports canary deployments, weighted traffic splitting, header-based routing, and cross-namespace routing natively — features that require complex annotations in Ingress.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1.5 hours🚀 Next-Gen

22.1 Why Gateway API Replaces Ingress

Ingress has critical limitations: no cross-namespace routing, no weighted traffic splitting, no header-based routing, no team role separation.

Ingress vs Gateway API — Resource Ownership Model
  INGRESS (everyone edits one resource):
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Ingress Resource                           │
  │  (Platform team & App devs edit same thing) │
  │  • Host routing • Path routing • TLS config │
  │  ⚠️ No role separation!                     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  GATEWAY API (separation of concerns):
  ┌──────────────────────┐  ← Platform Team (cluster-scoped)
  │ GatewayClass         │  "This cluster supports Envoy Gateway"
  └──────────┬───────────┘
             │ references
  ┌──────────┴───────────┐  ← Cluster Admin
  │ Gateway              │  "Listen on :443 with TLS cert X"
  │ (ns-scoped)          │  "Listen on :80 for HTTP"
  └──────────┬───────────┘
             │ parentRefs
  ┌──────────┴───────────┐  ← App Developer (their namespace)
  │ HTTPRoute            │  "Route /api/* → jobpost-svc (90/10)"
  │ (ns-scoped)          │  App devs manage their OWN routes!
  └──────────┬───────────┘
             │
  ┌──────────┴───────────┐  ← Opt-in cross-namespace access
  │ ReferenceGrant       │  "Ns B allows Ns A to reference its
  │ (in target namespace)│   Services" — explicit opt-in only

22.2 Gateway API Resource Model — The Full Hierarchy

Gateway API — Complete Resource Tree with Relationships
  GatewayClass (cluster-scoped)
  │  spec.controllerName: "gateway.envoyproxy.io/gatewayclass-controller"
  │
  └──▶ Gateway (namespace: anihpj)
       │  spec.listeners:
       │    - {name: https, port: 443, protocol: HTTPS,
       │       tls: {mode: Terminate, certificateRefs: [anihpj-tls]}}
       │    - {name: http, port: 80, protocol: HTTP}
       │
       └──▶ HTTPRoute (namespace: anihpj)
            │  spec.parentRefs: [{name: anihpj-gateway}]
            │  spec.rules:
            │    - matches: [{path: {type: PathPrefix, value: /api}}]
            │      backendRefs:
            │        - {name: jobpost-v1, port: 8000, weight: 90}
            │        - {name: jobpost-v2, port: 8000, weight: 10}
            │    - matches: [{path: {type: PathPrefix, value: /admin}}]
            │      backendRefs:
            │        - {name: admin, port: 8001}
            │
            └──▶ ReferenceGrant (namespace: database-ns)
                 │  "Allow HTTPRoutes in anihpj namespace to
                 │   reference Services in database-ns namespace"

22.3 Gateway API Implementations

💡 xDS — The Protocol That Makes Dynamic Reconfiguration Possible

Envoy-based gateways (Envoy Gateway, Istio, Contour) use the xDS (Discovery Service) protocol to dynamically update proxy configuration without reloads. xDS is a family of gRPC streaming APIs: LDS (Listener Discovery — "what ports to listen on"), RDS (Route Discovery — "which routes to which backends"), CDS (Cluster Discovery — "what backends exist"), EDS (Endpoint Discovery — "which IP:port for each backend"). The control plane streams configuration to Envoy over a persistent gRPC connection. When a backend pod starts/dies, only the EDS update is sent — Envoy adjusts its load balancing pool without dropping connections. This is fundamentally different from nginx's file-based reload model. xDS is why Envoy-based solutions can handle thousands of configuration changes per second without downtime.

ImplementationData PlaneKey StrengthMaturityeBPF?
Envoy GatewayEnvoyCNCF project, pure xDS, no reloadsGA (2024)❌
IstioEnvoyFull mesh + Gateway API, ALL route typesProduction❌
NGINX Gateway FabricNGINXFamiliar nginx, production-gradeGA (2024)❌
CiliumeBPF (kernel)Sidecarless, kernel-native routingProduction✅
TraefikTraefikDashboard, middleware, easiest startProduction❌
KongKong/OpenRestyPlugin ecosystem (300+ plugins)Production❌

Choosing: Already using Istio for service mesh? → Gateway API comes free. Want maximum performance? → Cilium eBPF eliminates sidecar overhead. Want simplicity? → Envoy Gateway or Traefik. Need plugins? → Kong. Familiar with nginx? → NGINX Gateway Fabric.

22.4 Gateway API in Practice — anihpj Canary Deployment

# HTTPRoute: 90% → jobpost-v1, 10% → jobpost-v2
apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: HTTPRoute
metadata:
  name: jobpost
spec:
  parentRefs:
    - name: anihpj-gateway
  rules:
    - matches:
        - path: {type: PathPrefix, value: /api}
      backendRefs:
        - name: jobpost-v1
          port: 8000
          weight: 90
        - name: jobpost-v2
          port: 8000
          weight: 10
    # Header-based override for testing
    - matches:
        - path: {type: PathPrefix, value: /api}
          headers:
            - name: x-canary
              value: "true"
      backendRefs:
        - name: jobpost-v2
          port: 8000

22.5 [LAB 🛠️] Gateway API Lab

kubectl get gatewayclass                     # Available implementations
kubectl apply -f gateway.yaml                # Create Gateway + HTTPRoute
kubectl get gateway -A                        # All gateways
kubectl get httproute -A                      # All routes
curl -H "Host: jobpost.anihpj.com" http://$GATEWAY_IP/api/jobs/
curl -H "x-canary: true" http://$GATEWAY_IP/api/jobs/  # Test canary

📝 Chapter 22 — Networking Practice Questions

Q1
Why was Gateway API created? What are the 3 main limitations of Ingress that it solves?
Show Answer
Answer

(1) No role separation: Ingress is all-or-nothing — app devs and platform teams edit the same resource. Gateway API separates GatewayClass (platform), Gateway (admin), HTTPRoute (developer). (2) Limited routing: Ingress can only route by host+path. Gateway API supports headers, query params, method, weighted splitting, mirroring. (3) No cross-namespace routing: Ingress can only reference Services in the same namespace. Gateway API supports cross-namespace references with ReferenceGrant for explicit permission.

Q2
Explain the Gateway API hierarchy: GatewayClass → Gateway → HTTPRoute. Who creates each?
Show Answer
Answer

GatewayClass (platform team): "This cluster supports Envoy Gateway as an implementation." Cluster-scoped. Gateway (cluster admin): "This Gateway listens on port 443 with this TLS cert, in namespace X." Defines listeners. HTTPRoute (app developer): "Requests to /api/* with header x-version=v2 go to Service jobpost-v2." Defines routing rules in their own namespace. This separation means app devs can manage their own routes without touching infrastructure config.

Q3
How does Gateway API support canary deployments? How is this different from Ingress?
Show Answer
Answer

Gateway API HTTPRoute supports weighted backendRefs natively: weight: 90 for v1, weight: 10 for v2. Ingress requires complex workarounds (multiple Ingresses with canary annotations, nginx-specific). Gateway API can also route based on headers (x-canary: true → v2, otherwise → v1), enabling A/B testing without weight splitting. These are first-class features in the API spec, not vendor-specific annotations.

Q4
What is a ReferenceGrant and why is it needed?
Show Answer
Answer

ReferenceGrant is an explicit permission model for cross-namespace routing. Without it, an HTTPRoute in namespace A could not reference a Service in namespace B (security isolation).
