With a ReferenceGrant in namespace B saying "I allow namespace A to reference my Service X," cross-namespace routing becomes possible but ONLY with explicit opt-in. This prevents namespace isolation bypass — you can't accidentally route to another team's service.

Q5
Name 3 Gateway API implementations and their key differentiators.
Show Answer
Answer

(1) Envoy Gateway: CNCF project, xDS dynamic config, no reloads. (2) Istio Gateway API: Full service mesh + Gateway API in one, supports ALL route types (HTTP, gRPC, TCP, UDP, TLS). (3) Cilium Gateway API: eBPF-native, no sidecars, runs in the kernel. (4) NGINX Gateway Fabric: Familiar nginx, production-grade. (5) Traefik: Dashboard, middleware, easiest to get started.

Q6
What route types does Gateway API support besides HTTP?
Show Answer
Answer

Gateway API supports multiple route types: HTTPRoute (HTTP/HTTPS), GRPCRoute (gRPC), TLSRoute (TLS passthrough — SNI-based routing), TCPRoute (L4 TCP routing), UDPRoute (L4 UDP routing). This makes Gateway API usable for non-HTTP workloads — something Ingress can't do. Istio is the only implementation that currently supports all route types.

Q7
How does Gateway API handle TLS differently from Ingress?
Show Answer
Answer

Ingress: TLS is a list of hosts+secretName under spec.tls. Gateway API: TLS is configured on the Gateway's listeners. Each listener can have its own TLS mode (Terminate or Passthrough), certificate references, and allowed routes. This is more flexible: one Gateway can terminate TLS for api.example.com while passing through TLS for mTLS.example.com — different listeners, different TLS behavior. cert-manager 1.13+ supports Gateway API natively.

Q8
You have an existing NGINX Ingress setup with 50 Ingress resources. How would you migrate to Gateway API?
Show Answer
Answer

Gateway API and Ingress can coexist. (1) Install NGINX Gateway Fabric (or your chosen implementation). (2) Create GatewayClass and Gateway. (3) Create HTTPRoutes for new services. (4) Gradually migrate existing Ingresses to HTTPRoutes (they can coexist during migration). (5) Once all traffic is on Gateway API, delete old Ingresses. Tools like ingress2gateway can auto-convert Ingress resources to Gateway API resources. No downtime migration is possible because both can run simultaneously.

Q9
What happens if you create a Gateway but no GatewayClass exists?
Show Answer
Answer

The Gateway will be stuck in a "NotReady" state with a condition: "GatewayClassNotAccepted" or "NoResources." The Gateway references a GatewayClass — if that GatewayClass doesn't exist, no controller will pick up the Gateway. It's like an Ingress without an Ingress Controller. The Gateway remains as configuration but has no effect until the GatewayClass (and its controller) is installed and running.

Q10
Design a Gateway API configuration for anihpj: TLS termination, /api/* → jobpost (weighted 90/10 canary), /admin/* → admin.
Show Answer
Answer
# Gateway with TLS
apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Gateway
metadata: {name: anihpj-gateway}
spec:
  gatewayClassName: envoy
  listeners:
    - name: https
      port: 443
      protocol: HTTPS
      tls:
        mode: Terminate
        certificateRefs:
          - name: anihpj-tls
      allowedRoutes:
        namespaces: {from: Same}
---
# HTTPRoute with canary + path routing
apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: HTTPRoute
metadata: {name: anihpj-routes}
spec:
  parentRefs: [{name: anihpj-gateway}]
  rules:
    - matches: [{path: {type: PathPrefix, value: /api}}]
      backendRefs:
        - {name: jobpost-v1, port: 8000, weight: 90}
        - {name: jobpost-v2, port: 8000, weight: 10}
    - matches: [{path: {type: PathPrefix, value: /admin}}]
      backendRefs:
        - {name: admin, port: 8001}

Chapter 23: Service Mesh — The Next Layer of Networking

A service mesh moves networking concerns (retries, timeouts, mTLS, observability) out of application code into infrastructure. Every pod gets a sidecar proxy (Envoy or Linkerd-proxy) injected. The sidecar intercepts all traffic and applies policies — without the application knowing. This gives you mTLS, traffic management, and observability with zero code changes.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1.5 hours🕸️ Mesh

23.1 What Is a Service Mesh and Why Does It Exist?

Without a mesh, every team re-implements: retries, timeouts, circuit breaking, mTLS, distributed tracing, metrics. A service mesh extracts these into the infrastructure layer. Data plane: Sidecar proxies (Envoy/Linkerd-proxy) handle all traffic. Control plane: Istiod/Linkerd controller distributes configuration. How interception works: An init container runs iptables rules to redirect ALL pod traffic through the sidecar proxy (port 15001 for Istio).

⚠️ If the sidecar crashes, the pod is completely isolated

The init container redirects ALL traffic through the sidecar. If the sidecar stops, iptables rules still redirect to a dead proxy → all connections fail. The pod appears "Running" but has zero network connectivity. This is the #1 service mesh debugging gotcha.

💡 How Sidecar Injection Works — Step by Step

(1) Namespace labeled istio-injection=enabled → Istio mutating webhook triggers on pod creation. (2) Webhook modifies pod spec: adds init container (runs iptables redirect), adds Envoy sidecar container, mounts certificates. (3) Init container runs first: iptables -t nat -A PREROUTING -p tcp -j REDIRECT --to-port 15001 (redirects ALL inbound/outbound pod traffic to Envoy). (4) Init container also redirects DNS to Envoy for service discovery. (5) Application container starts — all its traffic is transparently intercepted. (6) Envoy sidecar connects to Istiod (control plane) via xDS, receives certificates (SPIFFE X.509), routing rules, and policies. The app NEVER knows it's being proxied — it makes HTTP calls as usual.

💡 SPIFFE — The Identity Framework Behind mTLS

SPIFFE (Secure Production Identity Framework for Everyone) is the CNCF standard that gives every service a cryptographic identity. In Istio, every sidecar gets a SPIFFE X.509 certificate with an identity like: spiffe://cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/jobpost (cluster/namespace/serviceAccount). This identity is NOT tied to IP or hostname — it's tied to the Kubernetes ServiceAccount. When jobpost calls postgres, the postgres sidecar verifies: "Is spiffe://cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/jobpost allowed to call me?" Certificates are automatically rotated (default: every 24 hours) by Istiod. The private key never leaves the pod. Why this matters: mTLS isn't just encryption — it's cryptographically proving WHO is calling. Without SPIFFE, mTLS just proves "someone with a valid cert is calling." With SPIFFE, it proves "the jobpost service in namespace anihpj is calling." This is the foundation of zero-trust: every request is authenticated, not just encrypted.

23.2 Service Mesh Capabilities

mTLS: Automatic, transparent mutual TLS between all services. Traffic splitting: Canary deployments, blue/green, A/B testing.

💡 Traffic Management — Retries, Timeouts, Circuit Breaking, Fault Injection Explained

These four features are the building blocks of resilient microservices — and the mesh provides them without application code changes. Retries: If a backend returns a transient error (connection refused, 503), the sidecar automatically retries up to N times with exponential backoff. The app code doesn't need try/catch/retry logic. ⚠️ Retries AMPLIFY load: If every service retries 3× on failure, total requests can triple during an outage. Always configure retry budgets (max retries × request rate). Timeouts: How long the sidecar waits before giving up. Without timeouts, a slow backend can exhaust all your gunicorn workers waiting for responses. Set timeouts slightly below your application's tolerance (e.g., 25s mesh timeout for a 30s app timeout). Circuit Breaking: If a backend returns N consecutive 5xx errors, the circuit "opens" — the sidecar immediately returns 503 without even sending the request. This protects the failing backend from overload while it recovers. After a cooldown, the breaker "half-opens" — allows one test request through. Fault Injection: Intentionally inject delays or errors to test resilience. "What happens if postgres takes 5 seconds to respond?" Inject a 5s delay for 10% of traffic and observe. This is chaos engineering built into the mesh — test failure scenarios safely in production.

💡 RED Metrics — How the Mesh Gives You Observability for Free

RED stands for the three golden signals of service health: Rate (requests per second — is traffic normal?), Errors (percentage of failed requests — are users seeing 5xx?), Duration (latency — is the service getting slower?). The sidecar proxy (Envoy/Linkerd) automatically collects these for EVERY request without code changes. They're exported to Prometheus, visualized in Grafana/Kiali as service graphs with color-coded health (green=healthy, red=errors, orange=latency). The power: Deploy a mesh → instantly get: per-service RPS, error rate, p50/p95/p99 latency, request volume by HTTP status code. This is "zero-code observability."

Istio vs Linkerd vs Cilium: Istio = maximum features, higher complexity. Linkerd = simpler, Rust-based proxy, lower overhead. Cilium = eBPF-native, no sidecars at all (sidecarless mesh).

23.3 Service Mesh in the anihpj Stack

anihpj with Istio — mTLS, Canary, Circuit Breaker, Tracing
  INGRESS GATEWAY (Istio) — TLS termination + routing
  │
  ├──▶ JOBOOST POD                             POSTGRES POD
  │    ┌─────────────┐                          ┌─────────────┐
  │    │  jobpost    │──HTTP──▶┌─────────────┐│  postgres   │
  │    │  container  │         │  ENVOY      ││  container  │
  │    │  localhost  │◀──HTTP──│  SIDECAR    ││  :5432      │
  │    │  :8000      │         │             ││             │
  │    └─────────────┘         │ • mTLS 🔒   │└─────────────┘
  │                            │ • Circuit   │
  │  90% → jobpost-v1          │   Breaker   │  REDIS POD
  │  10% → jobpost-v2 (canary) │ • Retries   │  ┌─────────────┐
  │                            │ • Timeout   │  │   redis     │
  │                            │ • Tracing   │  │  container  │
  │                            │ • Metrics   │  └─────────────┘
  │                            └──────┬──────┘
  │                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┐
  │  │        TELEMETRY               ▼                      │
  │  │  Prometheus ← metrics    "GET /api/jobs/ → 200 (45ms)│
  │  │  Jaeger     ← traces     Trace: nginx(10ms)→          │
  │  │  Kiali      ← graph      jobpost(45ms)→postgres(30ms)│
  │  │  Grafana    ← dashboard  →redis(2ms)"                │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  CIRCUIT BREAKER (protecting postgres):
  maxConnections: 5     → max 5 concurrent DB connections
  consecutive5xx: 5     → eject after 5 straight failures
  ejectionTime: 30s     → wait 30s before retrying backend

23.4 [LAB 🛠️] Service Mesh Lab

# Install Istio
istioctl install --set profile=demo
kubectl label namespace anihpj istio-injection=enabled
# Redeploy pods — they now have Envoy sidecars
kubectl get pods -n anihpj   # 2/2 containers (app + istio-proxy)
kubectl exec deploy/jobpost -c istio-proxy -- curl localhost:15000/config_dump
# Enable mTLS: apply PeerAuthentication STRICT
# Verify: tcpdump on any pod interface — traffic is encrypted
# Kiali dashboard: istioctl dashboard kiali

📝 Chapter 23 — Networking Practice Questions

Q1
What is a service mesh? What problem does it solve that Kubernetes Services don't?
Show Answer
Answer

A service mesh moves networking concerns (mTLS, retries, timeouts, circuit breaking, traffic splitting, observability) out of application code into infrastructure sidecar proxies. K8s Services provide: L4 load balancing + service discovery. Service mesh adds: Automatic mTLS, L7 traffic management, distributed tracing, canary deployments, fault injection, circuit breaking. Services handle "where to send." Mesh handles "how to send" — reliability, security, observability.

Q2
How does the Istio sidecar intercept pod traffic? What happens if the sidecar crashes?
Show Answer
Answer

An init container runs iptables -t nat -A PREROUTING -p tcp -j REDIRECT --to-port 15001, redirecting all inbound/outbound pod traffic to the Envoy sidecar on port 15001. Envoy then forwards to the actual destination (after applying policies). If sidecar crashes: iptables rules still redirect to dead proxy → ALL connections fail. Pod appears Running but has NO network. The sidecar is a critical single point of failure for that pod. This is why sidecar resource limits and liveness probes are essential.

Q3
What is mTLS in a service mesh? How is it different from regular TLS?
Show Answer
Answer

Regular TLS: Only server proves identity. Client is anonymous. mTLS: Both client AND server present certificates and prove identity. In a mesh, every service gets a certificate (SPIFFE X.509) from the control plane. Service A calls Service B → both sidecars present certs → mutually authenticated. This prevents: (1) unauthorized services calling your API (even within the cluster). (2) Man-in-the-middle attacks inside the cluster. (3) Impersonation — Service X can't pretend to be Service Y.

Q4
Compare Istio, Linkerd, and Cilium Service Mesh. When would you choose each?
Show Answer
Answer

Istio: Maximum features (ALL route types, Wasm extensibility, multi-cluster). Envoy sidecars. Higher complexity. Best for: large orgs that need everything. Linkerd: Simpler, Rust proxy (lower latency, smaller footprint). Fewer features but easier to operate. Best for: teams that want simplicity and performance. Cilium: Sidecarless — uses eBPF in the kernel. No proxy per pod. Best for: performance-critical workloads, teams already using Cilium as CNI. All three provide mTLS, traffic management, and observability.

Q5
What is circuit breaking in a service mesh? How would you configure it for the anihpj postgres connection?
Show Answer
Answer

Circuit breaking prevents cascading failures by detecting failing backends and stopping traffic to them. For anihpj→postgres: maxConnections: 5 (max concurrent connections to postgres). consecutive5xxErrors: 5 (if 5 consecutive 5xx errors, eject backend for 30s). interval: 10s (check every 10s). Why: If postgres slows down, connections pile up → gunicorn workers exhaust → jobpost becomes unresponsive. Circuit breaker detects this early and fails fast (returning 503) instead of letting connections queue up. This protects the entire system.

Q6
How does distributed tracing work in a service mesh? Walk through a trace for an anihpj API request.
Show Answer
Answer

The sidecar automatically injects trace headers (b3/Zipkin format) into every request. Flow: (1) Request arrives at Ingress → sidecar generates trace ID and span ID, adds headers: x-b3-traceid: abc123, x-b3-spanid: span1. (2) Forwards to jobpost → jobpost sidecar creates child span (span2), propagates headers. (3) jobpost queries postgres → sidecar creates span3. (4) postgres responds → span3 closed. (5) jobpost responds → span2 closed. (6) Response to client → span1 closed. Jaeger/Zipkin collects all spans and shows the full waterfall: Ingress(10ms) → jobpost(45ms) → postgres(30ms). This works with ZERO application code changes — the sidecars handle all tracing.

Q7
What is the data plane vs control plane in a service mesh? What does each do?
Show Answer
Answer

Data plane: The sidecar proxies (Envoy/Linkerd-proxy) in every pod. They handle ALL data traffic — forwarding, load balancing, mTLS, retries, circuit breaking, telemetry collection. Control plane: Istiod/Linkerd controller. It: (1) Issues certificates to sidecars. (2) Distributes routing/config/rules to sidecars via xDS API. (3) Collects telemetry. (4) Manages service discovery. The data plane operates independently — if the control plane goes down, existing connections continue to work (data plane has cached config). New sidecars won't get certs until control plane recovers.

Q8
How do you verify that mTLS is actually working between two services in Istio?
Show Answer
Answer
# Check PeerAuthentication policy (STRICT = mTLS required)
kubectl get peerauthentication -A
# Check if sidecar reports mTLS
kubectl exec deploy/jobpost -c istio-proxy -- curl localhost:15000/config_dump | grep -A5 "ssl_context"
# tcpdump shows encrypted traffic:
kubectl exec deploy/jobpost -c istio-proxy -- tcpdump -i eth0 -A | grep "TLS"
# Istioctl verify:
istioctl authn tls-check jobpost-xxx.anihpj postgres.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
# Kiali dashboard shows lock icons on mTLS-secured connections
Q9
What are the main differences between sidecar-based mesh (Istio/Linkerd) and sidecarless mesh (Cilium)?
Show Answer
Answer

Sidecar (Istio/Linkerd): Every pod gets a proxy container. Traffic goes app→sidecar→network→sidecar→app. Pros: Language-agnostic, works with any app. Cons: Resource overhead (extra CPU/RAM per pod), latency (extra hop), operational complexity (sidecar injection, upgrades). Sidecarless (Cilium): eBPF programs in the kernel handle mesh functions. No proxy per pod. Pros: Zero sidecar overhead, lower latency, simpler operations. Cons: Requires eBPF-capable kernel (Linux 5.10+), fewer L7 features than Envoy, newer/less mature.

Q10
You deployed Istio and suddenly all pods can't communicate. What went wrong?
Show Answer
Answer

Most likely causes: (1) PeerAuthentication set to STRICT but sidecars not fully ready: Pods started before sidecars got certificates → mTLS fails → connections dropped. Fix: wait for sidecars to be ready, or deploy mesh in PERMISSIVE mode first. (2) Sidecar resource limits too low: Sidecar OOMKilled → iptables still redirects to dead proxy → zero connectivity. (3) Istio CNI plugin not installed: Init container requires NET_ADMIN capability for iptables. Without Istio CNI, pods need elevated privileges. (4) MTU mismatch: Sidecar adds overhead to packets. If not configured for overlay network MTU, packets are dropped.

☁️ Part 7: Cloud Networking — VPCs, Subnets & Hybrid ConnectivityCh 24-26

Chapter 24: Virtual Private Cloud (VPC) Fundamentals

A Virtual Private Cloud (VPC) is your own logically isolated section of the cloud — a virtual data center where you control IP addressing, subnets, routing, and security. Every cloud resource (EC2, RDS, EKS) lives inside a VPC. Understanding VPC design is essential for building secure, scalable cloud architectures.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~2 hours☁️ Cloud🏗️ Architecture

24.1 What Is a VPC?

A VPC is defined by a CIDR block (e.g., 10.0.0.0/16 = 65,536 IPs), divided into subnets (e.g., 10.0.1.0/24 = 256 IPs per subnet) across Availability Zones for high availability. Route tables control traffic flow between subnets and to the internet. Gateways (Internet Gateway, NAT Gateway) provide internet access.

AWS VPC Architecture — anihpj Production Design
                    AWS VPC: 10.0.0.0/16

  ┌──────────────────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │  PUBLIC SUBNET (10.0.1.0/24) │  │  PUBLIC SUBNET (10.0.2.0/24) │
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌───────────┐ │  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌───────────┐ │
  │  │ Bastion  │ │ NAT GW    │ │  │  │ NAT GW   │ │  ALB     │ │
  │  │ Host     │ │ (AZ-a)    │ │  │  │ (AZ-b)   │ │ (public)  │ │
  │  └──────────┘ └─────┬─────┘ │  │  └─────┬────┘ └─────┬─────┘ │
  └─────────────────────┼───────┘  └────────┼────────────┼───────┘
                        │                   │            │
  ┌─────────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────┼───────┐
  │  PRIVATE SUBNET (10.0.10.0/24 — AZ-a)   │            │       │
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ │            │       │
  │  │EKS Node │ │EKS Node │ │   RDS    │◀─┘ (route to NAT)     │
  │  │(jobpost)│ │(jobpost)│ │PostgreSQL│                        │
  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘                        │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Internet Gateway ──▶ 0.0.0.0/0 (public subnets)
  NAT Gateway ──▶ 0.0.0.0/0 (private subnets → outbound internet only)

24.2 Public vs Private Subnets — The Great Divide

The fundamental security boundary: Public subnet: Route to Internet Gateway — resources CAN receive inbound internet traffic. Private subnet: Route to NAT Gateway — outbound-only internet access.

Public vs Private Subnet — Route Table Comparison
  PUBLIC SUBNET ROUTE TABLE:              PRIVATE SUBNET ROUTE TABLE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ 10.0.0.0/16 → local         │        │ 10.0.0.0/16 → local         │
  │ 0.0.0.0/0   → IGW           │        │ 0.0.0.0/0   → NAT Gateway   │
  └──────────────────────────────┘        └──────────────────────────────┘
  ✅ Resources CAN have public IPs        ❌ Resources have private IPs only
  ✅ Internet CAN initiate connections     ✅ Resources can initiate OUTBOUND
  ✅ Traffic flows: Internet ↔ Subnet     ❌ Internet CANNOT initiate inbound
                                           💡 NAT GW translates private IP→public

  RULE OF THUMB: Public = Load Balancers, Bastions. Private = Everything else.
  NAT Gateway: ~$0.045/GB + $0.045/hr. Use VPC Endpoints to reduce costs!

24.3 Security Groups vs Network ACLs

FeatureSecurity Group (SG)Network ACL (NACL)
ScopeInstance/ENI levelSubnet level
StateStateful (return traffic auto-allowed)Stateless (must allow return traffic)
RulesAllow onlyAllow AND Deny
EvaluationAll rules evaluated before decisionRules evaluated in order (lowest number first)
DefaultDeny all inbound, allow all outboundAllow all inbound + outbound
💡 How They Work Together

Traffic hits the NACL FIRST (at the subnet boundary), then the Security Group (at the instance). Both must allow the traffic. NACLs are your first line of defense — use them to block known bad IPs at the subnet level. SGs are your per-instance firewall — use them to allow specific application ports. SGs can reference other SGs as sources (e.g., "allow all traffic from the EKS-node SG").

24.4 ENIs (Elastic Network Interfaces) — Cloud NICs Explained

An ENI is a virtual NIC — MAC address, private IP(s), Security Groups attached.

EC2 with 2 ENIs — Node Primary + Pod Secondary IPs (AWS VPC CNI)
  EC2 INSTANCE (t3.medium — EKS worker node)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────┐     │
  │  │ PRIMARY ENI (eth0)   │    │ SECONDARY ENI (eth1) │     │
  │  │ Primary IP:          │    │ Primary IP:          │     │
  │  │ 10.0.10.25 (node)    │    │ 10.0.10.30           │     │
  │  │ Secondary IPs:       │    │ Secondary IPs:       │     │
  │  │ 10.0.10.26 (pod-1)   │    │ 10.0.10.31 (pod-3)   │     │
  │  │ 10.0.10.27 (pod-2)   │    │ 10.0.10.32 (pod-4)   │     │
  │  └──────────────────────┘    └──────────────────────┘     │
  │  Total pod IPs: 4 (2 ENIs × 3 IPs - 2 primary = 4 pods)  │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  INSTANCE   │ ENIs │ IPs/ENI │ Max Pods
  ───────────┼──────┼─────────┼──────────
  t3.medium  │  2   │    3    │   4  ⚠️
  t3.large   │  3   │   12    │  34
  m5.large   │  3   │   10    │  28
  c5.4xlarge │  8   │   30    │ 234
  ⚠️ t3.medium = only 4 pods! Use larger instances or Calico/Cilium.

24.5 VPC Peering, Transit Gateway, and PrivateLink

🔗
VPC Peering

Direct connection between two VPCs using AWS backbone. Non-transitive — VPC A peered with B, B peered with C does NOT mean A can reach C. Works across accounts and regions. Doesn't scale beyond a few VPCs — the mesh of peering connections becomes unmanageable.

⭐
Transit Gateway

Hub-and-spoke model — connects thousands of VPCs through a central hub. Transitive by design — all VPCs can communicate through the TGW. Supports VPN and Direct Connect attachments. The modern way to connect multi-VPC architectures.

🔒
PrivateLink

Expose a service (behind an NLB) to other VPCs without traffic leaving the AWS backbone — no internet, no VPC peering. Consumer creates an endpoint ENI in their VPC → traffic flows to producer's NLB. Used for SaaS (vendor exposes API via PrivateLink) and internal shared services.

🛡️
Gateway Endpoints

Free, highly available endpoints for S3 and DynamoDB. Instead of traffic going through NAT Gateway ($$$ per GB), it goes through the gateway endpoint directly to S3/DynamoDB over AWS backbone — no internet, no NAT charges. Always use these for production workloads.

📝 Chapter 24 — Networking Practice Questions

Q1
What is a VPC? What are its core components?
Show Answer
Answer

A VPC is a logically isolated section of the cloud defined by a CIDR block (e.g., 10.0.0.0/16). Core components: Subnets (IP ranges in AZs), Route Tables (traffic direction), Internet Gateway (public internet access), NAT Gateway (outbound internet for private subnets), Security Groups (instance firewall), NACLs (subnet firewall). This is your virtual data center — every cloud resource lives in a VPC.

Q2
What's the difference between a public subnet and a private subnet?
Show Answer
Answer

Public subnet: Route table has a route to an Internet Gateway (0.0.0.0/0 → IGW). Resources can have public IPs and receive inbound internet traffic. Private subnet: No route to IGW. Resources use NAT Gateway for outbound internet only. Cannot receive inbound internet traffic directly. Rule: LBs in public, everything else in private.

Q3
Compare Security Groups and Network ACLs. When would you use each?
Show Answer
Answer

SGs: Stateful, instance-level, allow-only rules. NACLs: Stateless, subnet-level, allow+deny rules, evaluated in order. Use NACLs: Block known malicious IPs at subnet boundary, enforce org-wide deny rules. Use SGs: Per-instance application firewall, reference other SGs (allow EKS-node SG to talk to RDS SG). Both must allow traffic — NACL first, then SG.

Q4
What is an ENI? How does AWS EKS use ENIs for pod networking?
Show Answer
Answer

An ENI is a virtual NIC with its own MAC, IP(s), and SGs. EKS (AWS VPC CNI): Pods get IPs from ENI secondary IP addresses — not from an overlay. Each instance type has ENI limits: t3.medium = 2 ENIs × 3 IPs = 6, minus 2 primary = 4 pod IPs max. For more pods: larger instances, prefix delegation, or switch to Calico/Cilium with overlay. The benefit: pod IPs are real VPC IPs — no encapsulation overhead.

Q5
What is the difference between VPC Peering, Transit Gateway, and PrivateLink?
Show Answer
Answer

Peering: 1:1 VPC connection, non-transitive (A→B + B→C ≠ A→C). Good for 2-3 VPCs. Transit Gateway: Hub-and-spoke, transitive, supports 1000s of VPCs + VPN + Direct Connect. PrivateLink: Expose a single service (behind NLB) to other VPCs without any VPC-to-VPC connectivity — traffic stays on AWS backbone. Use PrivateLink for shared services; TGW for full network connectivity.

Q6
Your private subnet instances can't reach the internet. The NAT Gateway is running. What's wrong?
Show Answer
Answer

(1) Route table: private subnet needs 0.0.0.0/0 → NAT Gateway (not IGW!). (2) NAT Gateway must be in a PUBLIC subnet (needs IGW route to reach internet). (3) Security Group on the instance must allow outbound traffic. (4) NACL must allow outbound (ephemeral ports 1024-65535). (5) NAT Gateway may have been deleted/stopped. Check: aws ec2 describe-route-tables, verify the NAT Gateway's subnet has IGW route.

Q7
What is a Gateway Endpoint? Why should you always use one for S3/DynamoDB?
Show Answer
Answer

Gateway Endpoints provide private connectivity to S3 and DynamoDB without internet access. Without them, traffic from private subnets to S3 goes through NAT Gateway → internet → S3 (charging NAT per-GB fees). With a Gateway Endpoint, traffic goes directly to S3 over AWS backbone — FREE. Add a route: S3 prefix list → Gateway Endpoint. Always use for production — saves significant NAT Gateway costs on S3-heavy workloads (images, logs, backups).

Q8
Design the network layout for anihpj on AWS: EKS cluster, RDS, ElastiCache, public-facing API.
Show Answer
Answer

VPC: 10.0.0.0/16. Public subnets (2 AZs): ALB (internet-facing). Private app subnets (2 AZs): EKS worker nodes. Private data subnets (2 AZs): RDS + ElastiCache. Route tables: Public → IGW. Private app → NAT GW. Private data → NAT GW. Security: EKS-node SG allows from ALB SG. RDS SG allows from EKS-node SG. VPC endpoints: S3 (container images), ECR (image pulls). PrivateLink: none needed. TGW: connect to shared-services VPC for monitoring/logging.

Q9
Why do Security Groups support referencing other Security Groups as sources?
Show Answer
Answer

Instead of hardcoding IPs in SG rules (which change when instances restart), you can reference another SG as the source: "allow traffic from the EKS-node SG." This is dynamic — any instance with that SG is allowed, regardless of its IP. Critical for auto-scaling groups and K8s clusters where node IPs change constantly. Example: RDS SG allows port 5432 from source: sg-eks-nodes. Any EC2 instance with that SG can connect.

Q10
You need to expose a service in VPC A to VPC B without connecting the VPCs. How?
Show Answer
Answer

Use AWS PrivateLink. In VPC A (producer): Create an NLB in front of your service. Create a VPC Endpoint Service pointing to that NLB. In VPC B (consumer): Create a VPC Endpoint (Interface type) pointing to the Endpoint Service. Traffic flows: consumer → endpoint ENI in VPC B → AWS backbone → NLB in VPC A → service. No VPC peering. No internet. No overlapping CIDR issues. The service is accessed via a private DNS name or the endpoint's private IP.

Chapter 25: Cloud Load Balancers — Beyond the Basics

Cloud providers offer managed load balancers that go far beyond simple round-robin. AWS ALB/NLB, GCP HTTP(S) LB, and Azure Load Balancer provide automatic scaling, TLS termination, path-based routing, and deep integration with cloud-native services.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1 hour⚖️ Load Balancing

25.1 Layer 4 vs Layer 7 Cloud Load Balancers

FeatureAWS NLB (L4)AWS ALB (L7)GCP TCP LB (L4)GCP HTTP(S) LB (L7)
Works atTCP/UDP/TLSHTTP/HTTPSTCP/UDPHTTP/HTTPS
RoutingIP:Port (5-tuple)Path, Host, HeadersIP:PortURL map (path/host)
TLSPassthrough (or terminate)Terminate (ACM)PassthroughTerminate (managed certs)
Static IP✅ (per AZ)❌ (changes)✅✅ (global anycast)
WebSocket✅ (TCP)✅✅✅
gRPC✅ (TCP)✅✅✅ (HTTP/2)
Best ForNon-HTTP, static IP, extreme perfHTTP APIs, microservicesNon-HTTP, simpleGlobal HTTP, CDN-like
💡 NLB + ALB — The Power Combination

Many AWS architectures use NLB → ALB: NLB provides a static IP (important for IP allowlisting, DNS A records), forwards to ALB which does HTTP routing. NLB handles TCP/TLS at wire speed; ALB handles L7 routing. This gives you: static IP + path-based routing + WAF + Cognito auth — all in one architecture.

NLB → ALB Architecture — Static IP + HTTP Routing
  INTERNET → NLB (static IP 34.120.45.67, TCP proxy)
               │
               ▼
             ALB (internal, HTTP routing)
               │  /api/* → jobpost target group
               │  /admin/* → admin target group
               │  TLS termination (ACM cert)
               │  WAF + Cognito auth
               ▼
         ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐
         │Pod-1 │ │Pod-2 │ │Pod-3 │
         └──────┘ └──────┘ └──────┘

25.2 [LAB 🛠️] VPC & LB Lab

# Design anihpj VPC on paper:
# VPC CIDR, subnets per AZ, route tables, gateways

# Create an ALB pointing to EKS NodePort:
kubectl expose deployment jobpost --type=NodePort --port=8000
# Ingress → ALB via AWS Load Balancer Controller

# Verify:
curl http://<alb-dns>/api/jobs/
aws elbv2 describe-target-health --target-group-arn <arn>

📝 Chapter 25 — Networking Practice Questions

Q1
What's the difference between AWS NLB and ALB? When would you use each?
Show Answer
Answer

NLB (L4): TCP/UDP/TLS, preserves source IP, static IP per AZ, extreme performance. Use for: non-HTTP workloads, static IP requirements, TLS passthrough, gaming/UDP. ALB (L7): HTTP/HTTPS only, path/host routing, WAF integration, Cognito auth. Use for: REST APIs, microservices, any HTTP workload needing L7 features. Both can target pods directly in EKS (IP mode).

Q2
Why combine NLB and ALB? What does this architecture give you?
Show Answer
Answer

NLB → ALB gives you: NLB provides static IP (critical for DNS A records and IP allowlisting), wire-speed TCP/TLS handling. ALB provides HTTP routing, WAF, Cognito, path-based routing. NLB handles the internet-facing side (stable, fast); ALB handles the application side (flexible, feature-rich). In EKS, this is a common production pattern for internet-facing services.

Q3
How does AWS ALB integrate with EKS? What is IP mode?
Show Answer
Answer

AWS Load Balancer Controller watches K8s Ingress resources and provisions ALBs. IP mode: ALB targets pod IPs directly (via ENI secondary IPs) — no NodePort hop needed. Traffic: ALB → pod IP (directly). This eliminates the extra network hop and preserves source IP. Requires AWS VPC CNI (pods have VPC-routable IPs). ALB health checks target pods directly on their container port.

Q4
What is GCP's global HTTP(S) Load Balancer? How is it different from AWS ALB?
Show Answer
Answer

GCP's global HTTP(S) LB is a single anycast IP that routes traffic to the nearest healthy backend globally — it's a CDN-like LB. AWS ALB is regional (per-region IP). GCP also provides: Cloud CDN integrated, Google-managed TLS certs, URL maps for routing, Cloud Armor for WAF. For global applications, GCP's LB is simpler (one IP, global) while AWS requires Route53 latency-based routing + multiple regional ALBs.

Q5
How do cloud LBs handle health checks? What happens when a backend fails?
Show Answer
Answer

LB sends periodic health check requests (configurable path, interval, threshold). If N consecutive checks fail, backend is marked unhealthy and removed from rotation. Existing connections may be dropped or completed (depends on LB type and deregistration delay). In EKS: ALB health checks target pods directly. The readinessProbe controls whether a pod receives traffic via the Service/Endpoints. Both must pass: pod readiness + LB health check.

Q6
What is TLS termination at the cloud LB? What are the trade-offs?
Show Answer
Answer

The cloud LB decrypts TLS (using AWS Certificate Manager or uploaded certs), then forwards plain HTTP to backends. Pros: Simplified backend certificates (one cert at LB, not per instance), integrated with cloud cert management (auto-renewal), WAF can inspect decrypted traffic. Cons: Traffic is plaintext between LB and backend (network sniffing possible), compliance may require end-to-end encryption. Mitigate with: VPC-only traffic, security groups, or re-encrypt at backend (double TLS).

Q7
Your ALB returns 503 Service Unavailable. The EKS pods are running. What's wrong?
Show Answer
Answer

Health checks are failing. Check: (1) ALB health check path correct? (2) Health check port matches container port? (3) Security Group on EKS nodes allows traffic from ALB SG? (4) Pod readiness probe passing? (5) Target group shows "unhealthy" — check the reason code in AWS console. Common: health check path returns 404 (wrong path), security group blocks ALB → node traffic, or pod is not ready (readinessProbe failing).

Q8
How do you make an internal-only ALB (not internet-facing)?
Show Answer
Answer

Set scheme: internal (instead of internet-facing). The ALB gets private IPs only — accessible from within the VPC, peered VPCs, or via Transit Gateway. Use for: internal APIs, microservice communication that needs L7 routing, services that shouldn't be internet-accessible. In EKS: the Ingress annotation alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/scheme: internal creates an internal ALB.

Q9
What is connection draining (deregistration delay) on a cloud LB?
Show Answer
Answer

When a backend is deregistered (scaling in, deployment, health failure), the LB stops sending NEW connections but keeps EXISTING connections open for a configurable time (default 300s). This allows in-flight requests to complete gracefully. In EKS: during rolling updates, the old pod is deregistered from the target group, LB drains connections, then pod is terminated. Too short = dropped connections. Too long = slow deployments. Set based on your max request duration.

Q10
Compare cloud load balancer architecture: AWS, GCP, and Azure. What are the key differences?
Show Answer
Answer

AWS: Separate NLB (L4) and ALB (L7) — you pick. Regional. GCP: Global HTTP(S) LB is L7, single anycast IP, routes to nearest region. TCP/UDP LB is regional. Azure: Load Balancer (L4), Application Gateway (L7 with WAF), Front Door (global HTTP). Key difference: GCP's global LB is truly global (one IP) — AWS requires Route53 + multiple regional ALBs for the same effect. Azure Front Door is similar to GCP's global LB.

Chapter 26: Hybrid Cloud Networking — Connecting On-Prem to Cloud

Most enterprises don't go all-cloud overnight. They operate in a hybrid model — some workloads on-premises, some in the cloud. Connecting these environments securely requires VPNs or dedicated connections. This chapter covers Site-to-Site VPN, Direct Connect, and hybrid architecture patterns.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1 hour🔗 Hybrid

26.1 VPNs — IPsec and WireGuard

Site-to-Site VPN connects your office network to a cloud VPC over an encrypted tunnel across the public internet.

Site-to-Site VPN — Office to Cloud VPC via IPsec Tunnel
  OFFICE (192.168.0.0/16)              AWS VPC (10.0.0.0/16)
  ┌──────────────────────┐    IPsec    ┌──────────────────────┐
  │ On-Prem Router       │═══╪══╪══╪═══│ Virtual Private GW   │
  │ (Customer Gateway)   │   AES-256  │ (VGW)                │
  │ Public: 203.x.x.x    │   Tunnel   │ Public: 52.x.x.x      │
  └──────────┬───────────┘            └──────────┬───────────┘
             │                                   │
  ┌──────────┴───────────┐            ┌──────────┴───────────┐
  │ DB: 192.168.1.50     │            │ EKS Node: 10.0.10.25 │
  └──────────────────────┘            └──────────────────────┘

  IPSEC: IKE (key exchange) → ESP (encrypted data tunnel)
  WIREGUARD: Curve25519 + ChaCha20, 4K lines, in-kernel since Linux 5.6
  AWS VPN: 1.25 Gbps/tunnel, 2 tunnels per connection (redundant)

26.2 Direct Connect / Cloud Interconnect

Direct Connect is a dedicated physical fiber connection from your data center to the cloud provider — no internet, consistent latency, guaranteed bandwidth.

VPN vs Direct Connect — Internet vs Dedicated Fiber
  VPN (public internet):                 DIRECT CONNECT (private fiber):
  Office ══ ISP ══ Internet ══ VPC       Office ═════ Fiber ═════ VPC
  Latency: 20-100ms (variable)           Latency: 2-5ms (consistent)
  BW: 1.25 Gbps max                      BW: 1/10/100 Gbps
  SLA: None                              SLA: 99.99%

  ANIHPJ HYBRID: DB on-prem (compliance), EKS in cloud (compute)
  ┌──────────────────────┐    Direct Connect 5ms   ┌──────────────────────┐
  │ PostgreSQL (primary) │═════════════════════════│ EKS (jobpost API)    │
  │ 192.168.1.50:5432    │                         │ 10.0.10.x pods       │
  └──────────────────────┘                         └──────────────────────┘
  Cost: ~$0.30/hr (1 Gbps port) + ~$0.02/GB egress (vs $0.09/GB internet)

📝 Chapter 26 — Networking Practice Questions

Q1
What is Site-to-Site VPN? How does IPsec create a secure tunnel?
Show Answer
Answer

Site-to-Site VPN connects two networks (office ↔ cloud VPC) over an encrypted IPsec tunnel. Phase 1 (IKE): Authenticate endpoints and negotiate encryption parameters → creates a secure control channel. Phase 2 (ESP): Negotiates the actual data encryption tunnel. All traffic between the two networks is encrypted using AES. Runs over the public internet but the contents are unreadable. AWS managed VPN: create Virtual Private Gateway, Customer Gateway, VPN Connection.

Q2
What's the difference between IPsec and WireGuard? When would you choose each?
Show Answer
Answer

IPsec: Veteran (1995), complex (400K+ lines), widely supported by network hardware. WireGuard: Modern (2018), simple (4K lines), in-kernel, faster, modern crypto (Curve25519). Choose IPsec: When you need hardware VPN appliance support, compliance requirements mandate IPsec, or you're connecting to cloud managed VPN services. Choose WireGuard: For software-to-software VPNs, maximum performance, simpler configuration, modern Linux/container environments. WireGuard is in the Linux kernel since 5.6.

Q3
What is AWS Direct Connect? When would you use it instead of VPN?
Show Answer
Answer

Direct Connect is a dedicated physical fiber connection from your data center to AWS (via a Direct Connect location). Use when: (1) You need consistent latency (not variable internet). (2) Bandwidth > 1.25 Gbps (VPN limit). (3) Regulatory compliance prohibits internet transit. (4) You want to reduce data egress costs. (5) SLA-backed uptime (99.99%) is required. Cost: Port hours + data transfer out. For most startups, VPN is sufficient. Direct Connect makes sense at enterprise scale or for latency-sensitive workloads.

Q4
What is Transit Gateway? How does it simplify hybrid networking?
Show Answer
Answer

Transit Gateway is a hub-and-spoke network transit center. Instead of mesh-peering VPCs individually, all VPCs connect to the TGW. TGW also supports VPN and Direct Connect attachments — so on-prem networks connect through the SAME hub. One TGW replaces: N×(N-1)/2 VPC peering connections, multiple VPN connections, and Direct Connect per VPC. TGW route tables control which VPCs can talk to each other. This is the standard architecture for multi-VPC, hybrid-cloud deployments.

Q5
Design a hybrid architecture: on-prem database + cloud compute for anihpj.
Show Answer
Answer

On-prem: PostgreSQL database (compliance requires data on-prem). Cloud: EKS cluster running jobpost API. Connectivity: Direct Connect (1 Gbps) from on-prem DC to AWS. TGW connects DC (via Direct Connect) to EKS VPC. EKS pods connect to on-prem DB via TGW route. Latency: ~5ms (Direct Connect is consistent; internet VPN could be 30-100ms variable). Backup: DMS replicates on-prem DB to RDS read replica for DR. DNS: Route53 resolver endpoints forward on-prem domain queries.

Q6
What is BGP? How does it relate to Direct Connect and VPN?
Show Answer
Answer

BGP (Border Gateway Protocol) is the routing protocol of the internet — it exchanges route information between networks. In Direct Connect: You establish BGP peering between your on-prem router and AWS. You advertise your on-prem prefixes; AWS advertises VPC prefixes. Both sides learn routes dynamically. In VPN: BGP (dynamic) or static routes can be used. BGP is preferred because routes update automatically when networks change. AWS managed VPN supports BGP by default.

Q7
Your on-prem database is slow when accessed from EKS. You have a Site-to-Site VPN. What can you do?
Show Answer
Answer

(1) Upgrade to Direct Connect: VPN runs over internet → variable latency. Direct Connect = consistent ~5ms. (2) Move database closer: Replicate on-prem DB to RDS in the same region as EKS → sub-ms latency. (3) Connection pooling: Use PgBouncer or RDS Proxy to minimize TCP handshake overhead across the VPN. (4) Query optimization: The network amplifies bad queries — N+1 queries that are fine at 1ms are terrible at 30ms. (5) Increase VPN bandwidth: Multiple tunnels with ECMP for higher aggregate throughput.

Q8
What are the security considerations for hybrid cloud networking?
Show Answer
Answer

Security considerations: (1) VPN/Direct Connect traffic should be encrypted (IPsec/WireGuard for VPN; Direct Connect is private but not encrypted by default — add MACsec or application TLS). (2) Network segmentation: Use TGW route tables to limit which VPCs can reach on-prem. (3) Least privilege: Only allow necessary ports (5432 for DB, not full access). (4) Monitoring: VPC Flow Logs + on-prem firewall logs to detect anomalies. (5) DNS: Route53 Resolver endpoints prevent DNS rebinding attacks. (6) Authentication: Don't rely on network security alone — use TLS + certificates for all cross-prem traffic.

Q9
What is AWS Client VPN? How is it different from Site-to-Site VPN?
Show Answer
Answer

Site-to-Site VPN: Connects entire NETWORKS (office → VPC). Configured between routers/firewalls. Always on. Client VPN: Connects individual USERS/DEVICES (laptop → VPC). Users install a VPN client, authenticate with certificates or SSO (SAML/OIDC). Used for: remote developers accessing private resources, admin access to private subnets. Managed OpenVPN-compatible service. Charges per active connection + per hour.

Q10
What are the key differences between AWS Direct Connect, GCP Cloud Interconnect, and Azure ExpressRoute?
Show Answer
Answer

All three provide dedicated physical connectivity to the cloud. Direct Connect: 1/10/100 Gbps, BGP routing, partners available (Equinix, etc.). Cloud Interconnect: Dedicated (10/100 Gbps) or Partner (50 Mbps-10 Gbps via provider). ExpressRoute: Microsoft's offering — also connects to Microsoft 365/Azure AD. Key differences: GCP offers partner interconnect at lower speeds; Azure uniquely connects to M365; AWS has widest partner ecosystem. All support BGP, VLANs, and SLA-backed uptime. The choice depends on your primary cloud provider.
